coming from
(m,\sigma)\in\Phi^{l}
(describing the word read before uncovering
s^{l}
)
and of
m^{\prime}
coming from a particular assumption
(m^{\prime},\tau)\in\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
(describing the word read after uncovering
s^{l}
, but before uncovering
s^{i}
).

{exa}

Suppose that
n=2
.
Let
\tau_{\mathsf{np}}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{1}\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
and
\tau_{\mathsf{pr}}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{1}\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
.
Consider the following elements of
{\mathcal{T}}^{0}
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=(p,\Psi^{2},\{(m_{1},\tau_{\mathsf{np}}),(m_{2},\tau_{\mathsf{pr}})\},\mathsf{np})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=(p,\Psi^{2},\{(m_{3},\tau_{\mathsf{np}})\},\mathsf{np})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(p,\Psi^{2},\{(m_{3},\tau_{\mathsf{np}})\},\mathsf{pr})\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}

\displaystyle=(q,\Phi^{2},\{(m_{4},\tau_{\mathsf{np}}),(m_{5},\tau_{\mathsf{pr}})\},\mathsf{pr})\,,

and the following elements of
{\mathcal{T}}^{1}
:

\displaystyle\xi_{1}

\displaystyle=(p,\Psi^{2},\mathsf{pr})\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{2}

\displaystyle=(p,\Psi^{2},\mathsf{np})\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{3}

\displaystyle=(q,\Phi^{2},\mathsf{pr})\,.

It holds that
\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma_{i})=\xi_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, and
\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma_{1}^{\prime})=\xi_{1}
.
We have composers resulting in a single pair from
M\times{\mathcal{T}}^{1}
, like

\displaystyle(\{(m_{6}\cdot m_{1},\tau_{\mathsf{np}}),(m_{6}\cdot m_{2},\tau_{\mathsf{pr}})\},\{(m_{6},\sigma_{1})\};\{(m_{6},\xi_{1})\};\mathsf{np})\,,

or

\displaystyle(\{(m_{6}\cdot m_{3},\tau_{\mathsf{np}})\},\{(m_{6},\sigma_{1}^{\prime})\};\{(m_{6},\xi_{1})\};\mathsf{np})\,.

Notice that these two composers have the same “output set”.
We may also repeat the same run descriptor with multiple monoid elements:

\displaystyle(\{(m_{6}\cdot m_{3},\tau_{\mathsf{np}}),(m_{7}\cdot m_{3},\tau_{\mathsf{np}})\},\{(m_{6},\sigma_{1}^{\prime}),(m_{7},\sigma_{1}^{\prime})\};\{(m_{6},\xi_{1}),(m_{7},\xi_{1})\};\mathsf{np})\,.

Here, the situation that
m_{6}\cdot m_{3}=m_{7}\cdot m_{3}
is allowed as well (and then the first set has only a single element).
Observe that that all the above composers are nonproductive, even though they involve productive run descriptors.
Next, we can also have composers involving multiple run descriptors, like

\displaystyle(\{(m_{6}\cdot m_{3},\tau_{\mathsf{np}})\},\{(m_{6},\sigma_{1}^{\prime}),(m_{6},\sigma_{2})\};\{(m_{6},\xi_{1}),(m_{6},\xi_{2})\};\mathsf{np})\,.

This composer is again nonproductive:
although there is a run descriptor
\tau_{\mathsf{np}}
that appears in assumption sets of both
\sigma_{1}^{\prime}
and
\sigma_{2}
, this run descriptor
\tau_{\mathsf{np}}
is nonproductive.
But if a productive run descriptor
\tau_{\mathsf{pr}}
appears in assumption sets of two run descriptors (like for
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{3}
), we have a productive composer:

\displaystyle(\{(m_{6}\cdot m_{1},\tau_{\mathsf{np}}),(m_{7}\cdot m_{4},\tau_{\mathsf{np}}),(m_{6}\cdot m_{2},\tau_{\mathsf{pr}}),(m_{7}\cdot m_{5},\tau_{\mathsf{pr}})\},\{(m_{6},\sigma_{1}),(m_{7},\sigma_{3})\};

\displaystyle\hskip-70.0001pt\{(m_{6},\xi_{1}),(m_{7},\xi_{3})\};\mathsf{pr})\,.

As negative examples, we have the following tuples, which are not composers:

\displaystyle(\{(m_{6}\cdot m_{3},\tau_{\mathsf{np}}),(m_{6}\cdot m_{2},\tau_{\mathsf{pr}})\},\{(m_{6},\sigma_{1}^{\prime})\};\{(m_{6},\xi_{1})\};\mathsf{np})\,,

and

\displaystyle(\{(m_{6}\cdot m_{1},\tau_{\mathsf{np}}),(m_{6}\cdot m_{2},\tau_{\mathsf{pr}}),(m_{7}\cdot m_{3},\tau_{\mathsf{np}})\},\{(m_{6},\sigma_{1}),(m_{7},\sigma_{1}^{\prime})\};

\displaystyle\hskip-40.00006pt\{(m_{6},\xi_{1}),(m_{7},\xi_{1})\};\mathsf{np})\,.

In the first tuple, the first set contains a redundant pair
(m_{6}\cdot m_{2},\tau_{\mathsf{pr}})
, which is forbidden.
In the second tuple, we have simultaneously two realizations of
\xi_{1}
: one using
\sigma_{1}
, and one using
\sigma_{1}^{\prime}
; this is forbidden as well.

In the next proposition we notice that composers are associative.

Proposition 14
.

Let
0\leq l\leq j\leq k\leq n
.
For all fixed
\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{l},\Psi^{k}
and
f\in\{\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}\}
, the following two conditions are equivalent:

•

(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{l};\Psi^{k};f)
is a composer, and

•

(\Phi^{j},\Phi^{j-1},\dots,\Phi^{l};\Psi^{j};f_{1})
and
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{j+1},\Psi^{j};\Psi^{k};f_{2})
are composers
for some
\Psi^{j}
and
f_{1},f_{2}
such that
(f=\mathsf{np})\Leftrightarrow(f_{1}=f_{2}=\mathsf{np})
.

Proof 7.1
.

We use symbols
X
and
X_{1},X_{2}
for the tuples from the first and second item, respectively.
First, notice that the definition of a composer instantiated either for
X
, or for both
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
simultaneously,
implies that
\Phi^{i}\subseteq M\times{\mathcal{T}}^{i}
for
i\in[l,k]
, and
\Psi^{k}\subseteq M\times{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
.
While proving the right-to-left implication, Condition
(C2)
of the definition of a composer instantiated for
X_{1}
implies that

\Psi^{j}=\{(m,\mathsf{red}^{j}(\sigma))\colon\,(m,\sigma)\in\Phi^{l}\}
.
While proving the opposite implication, we choose
\Psi^{j}
in this way; the effect is that
X_{1}
satisfies Condition
(C2)
.

We see that
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{red}^{j}(\sigma))=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
for
i\in[j+1,k]
and
\sigma\in{\mathcal{T}}^{l}
,
so Condition
(C1)
for
X
is equivalent to the conjunction of Condition
(C1)
for
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.

By Proposition
13
we have that
\mathsf{red}^{k}(\mathsf{red}^{j}(\sigma))=\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)
for
\sigma\in{\mathcal{T}}^{l}
, which implies that
Condition
(C2)
instantiated for
X
is equivalent to Condition
(C2)
instantiated for
X_{2}
.

Notice that
|\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})|\leq|\pi_{2}(\Psi^{j})|\leq|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})|
,
because
\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Psi^{j})\}
and
\pi_{2}(\Psi^{j})=\{\mathsf{red}^{j}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})\}
.
Thus
|\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})|=|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})|
if and only if
|\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})|=|\pi_{2}(\Psi^{j})|=|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})|
;
we obtain equivalence for Condition
(C3)
.

Finally, Condition
(C4)
for
X_{1}
says that
f_{1}=\mathsf{np}
if and only if

\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))\cap\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau))\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
for each
i\in[l+1,j]
and each
\sigma,\tau\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})
such that
\sigma\neq\tau
;
otherwise
f_{1}=\mathsf{pr}
.
For
X_{2}
and
f_{2}
we have the same with
i\in[j+1,k]
(here we use again the fact that
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{red}^{j}(\sigma))=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
).
Thus, while proving the right-to-left implication we have these equivalences for
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
; they imply that
(f=\mathsf{np})\Leftrightarrow(f_{1}=f_{2}=\mathsf{np})
.
While proving the left-to-right implication we define
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
so that this is satisfied;
then we have Condition
(C4)
for
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, and the equivalence
(f=\mathsf{np})\Leftrightarrow(f_{1}=f_{2}=\mathsf{np})
.

Derivation Trees

Next, we say when a run descriptor
\sigma
from
{\mathcal{T}}^{0}
can be assigned to a stack symbol
\gamma
.
To this end, we define how statements of the form
\gamma\vdash\sigma
can be derived.
Such a statement means that from a configuration with topmost stack symbol
\gamma
one can start a run described by the run descriptor
\sigma

(assuming that the stacks below
\gamma
satisfy the assumptions of
\sigma
).
Actually, it is not enough to define when
\gamma\vdash\sigma
is true;
we explicitly need to handle derivation trees justifying such statements.
Thus, in Definition
7.1
we define the notion of a
derivation tree for
\gamma\vdash\sigma
.
Having such a derivation tree
D
,
\gamma\vdash\sigma
is called the
conclusion
of
D
,
and
\sigma
is called the run descriptor of
D
and is denoted
\mathsf{rd}(D)
.

{defi}

We define the set of
derivation trees
as the smallest set satisfying the following conditions.
Let
p
be a state, and
\gamma
a stack symbol.

(1)

A triple
(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,p)
is a derivation tree for
\gamma\vdash(p,\emptyset,\emptyset,\dots,\emptyset,\mathsf{np})
.

(2)

Suppose that
\delta(\gamma,p)=\mathsf{read}(\vec{q})
and that
D^{\prime}
is a derivation tree for
\gamma\vdash\tau
, where the state of
\tau
is
\vec{q}(a)
for some
a\in A
.
Denote
\Phi^{i}=\varphi(a)\circ\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
for
i\in[1,n]
.
Then
(\mathsf{read},p,D^{\prime})
is a derivation tree for
\gamma\vdash(p,\Phi^{n},\Phi^{n-1},\dots,\Phi^{1},f)
, where
f=\mathsf{np}
if and only if
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
and
a\neq\sharp
.

(3)

Suppose that
\delta(\gamma,p)=(q,\mathsf{pop}^{k})
, and that
\tau^{k}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
is a run descriptor with state
q
.
Then
(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,p,\tau^{k})
is a derivation tree for

\displaystyle\gamma\vdash(p,\mathsf{ass}^{n}(\tau^{k}),\mathsf{ass}^{n-1}(\tau^{k}),\dots,\mathsf{ass}^{k+1}(\tau^{k}),\{(\mathbf{1}_{M},\tau^{k})\},\emptyset,\dots,\emptyset,\mathsf{np})\,.

(4)

Suppose that
\delta(\gamma,p)=(q,\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha})
, and that
D^{\prime}
is a derivation tree for
\alpha\vdash\tau
, where the state of
\tau
is
q
.
Denote
\Psi^{i}=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
for
i\in[1,n]
.
Moreover, suppose that
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{0};\Psi^{k};f)
is a composer,
and
\mathfrak{D}
is a set of derivation trees, all having the stack element
\gamma
in their conclusion, and such that
\{\mathsf{rd}(E)\colon\,E\in\mathfrak{D}\}=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
and
|\mathfrak{D}|=|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})|
.
Let

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Psi^{i}&\mbox{for }i\in[k+1,n]\,,\\
\Phi^{i}&\mbox{for }i=k\,,\\
\Psi^{i}\cup\Phi^{i}&\mbox{for }i\in[1,k-1]\,.\end{array}\right.

Then
(\mathsf{push},\gamma,p,D^{\prime},\mathfrak{D})
is a derivation tree for
\gamma\vdash(p,\Upsilon^{n},\Upsilon^{n-1},\dots,\Upsilon^{1},g)
,
where
g=\mathsf{np}
if and only if
f=\mathsf{np}
, and
\{\tau\}\cup\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
,
and
\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\cap\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
for each
i\in[1,k-1]
.

The
depth
of a derivation tree
D
, denoted
0pt(D)
, is defined naturally: it is
0
in Cases (
1
) and (
3
),

1+0pt(D^{\prime})
in Case (
2
), and
1+\max(0pt(D^{\prime}),\max_{E\in\mathfrak{D}}0pt(E))
in Case (
4
).

Notice that a derivation tree
D
determines its conclusion.
This can be seen by induction on the structure of
D
.
In Cases (
1
) and (
3
) this is immediate.
In Case (
2
),
\gamma
and
\tau
are determined by
D^{\prime}
, and the letter
a
is determined by
p
and by the state of
\tau

(recall that
\vec{q}
is required to be injective, by the definition of the
\mathsf{read}
operation);
this already fixes the whole conclusion of
D
. Case (
4
) is the most complicated one.
First, we can see that
\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
and all
\Psi^{i}
are fixed by
D^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{D}
.
Then, by the definition of a composer, we have that the set
\Phi^{0}
has to contain those pairs
(m,\tau)\in M\times\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
for which
(m,\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\in\Psi^{k}
.
The set
\Phi^{0}
fixes the rest of the composer, and thus the whole conclusion of
D
.

We now comment on the intuitions staying behind Definition
7.1
.
Let
D
be a derivation tree for
\gamma\vdash\sigma
, where the state of
\sigma
is
p
.
We should have in mind a run
R
with
R(0)=(p,s^{n}:s^{n-1}:\dots:s^{1}:u^{0}_{\gamma})
, where
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(u^{0}_{\gamma})=\gamma
.
Basically, the run descriptor
\sigma
describes such a run, and the derivation tree
D
specifies parts of this run for which the
0
-stack
u^{0}_{\gamma}
is responsible.

Case (
1
) corresponds to an empty run (
|R|=0
).
Thus, the assumption sets of
\sigma
are empty (stacks
s^{i}
are never uncovered), and the run descriptor is nonproductive.

In the remaining cases, nonempty runs are considered.
Case (
2
) talks about a run
R
starting with a
\mathsf{read}
operation.
Say that this operation reads a symbol
a
, and ends in a state
\vec{q}(a)
.
The derivation tree
D^{\prime}
(and, in particular, its run descriptor
\tau
) describes the suffix
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
.
Thus, we require that the state in
\tau
is
\vec{q}(a)
.
Because
R{\restriction}_{0,1}
does not modify the stack, assumptions from
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
, referring to
\mathsf{top}^{i}(\mathsf{pop}^{i}(R(1)))
,
refer simultaneously to
\mathsf{top}^{i}(\mathsf{pop}^{i}(R(0)))
(for
i\in[1,n]
).
This is expressed by the fact that as
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
we almost take assumptions from
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
.
The only difference is that we multiply the monoid element in every pair by
\varphi(a)
on the left.
This is because monoid elements in
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
correspond to some prefixes of
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
,
while monoid elements in
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
should talk about prefixes of the whole
R
;
the latter prefixes additionally read the symbol
a
at the very beginning.
Our new run descriptor
\sigma
is productive either when the first operation reads the
\sharp
symbol,
or when the run descriptor
\tau
, describing the rest of the run, is productive.
Recall that
\vec{q}
is required to be injective (cf. the definition of the
\mathsf{read}
operation), so seeing the state
\vec{q}(a)
we can determine the symbol
a
that was read.

In Case (
3
) the first operation of
R
is
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
, which leads to a configuration of the form
(q,s^{n}:s^{n-1}:\dots:s^{k})
.
The suffix
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
is described by a run descriptor
\tau^{k}
.
In particular, the state in
\tau^{k}
should be
q
.
For
i\in[1,k-1]
the
i
-stack
s^{i}
is never uncovered (it is destroyed by the
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
operation), so the assumption set
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
is set to
\emptyset
.
The
k
-stack
s^{k}
is uncovered after the first operation, so we put
\tau^{k}
to the assumption set
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)
,
together with the neutral element of the monoid (because the word read by
R{\restriction}_{0,1}
is empty).
This is the only pair in
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)
, because before
R(1)
no copies of
s^{k}
are created, so
s^{k}
cannot be uncovered again.
For
i\in[k+1,n]
, the assumption set
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau^{k})
talks about uncovering
s^{i}
, and hence it is taken as
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)

(the monoid elements remain unchanged, because
R{\restriction}_{0,1}
does not read any symbols).
Notice that, unlike for the
\mathsf{read}
operation, we do not include in
D
any derivation tree talking about
\tau^{k}
.
This is because in
D
we only describe the part of
R
for which the
0
-stack
u^{0}_{\gamma}
is responsible:
on the one hand, after the
\mathsf{read}
operation, the
0
-stack
u^{0}_{\gamma}
is still on the top of the stack and is responsible for continuing the run;
on the other hand, after the
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
operation,
u^{0}_{\gamma}
is no longer present on the stack.
The run descriptor
\sigma
is nonproductive:
u^{0}_{\gamma}
is not responsible for reading any
\sharp
symbol,
and every assumption of
\sigma
is used only once (for
i\in[k+1,n]
, we simply pass every assumption from
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
to
\tau^{k}
;
independently,
\tau^{k}
may use these assumptions more than once, but this is a responsibility of
\tau^{k}
, not of
\sigma
).

Finally, we have Case (
4
), where the first operation of
R
is
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
, leading to a configuration of the form

(q,s^{n}:s^{n-1}:\dots:s^{k+1}:t^{k}:\mathsf{p}_{+1}(s^{k-1}:s^{k-2}:\dots:s^{1}:u^{0}_{\alpha}))
with
t^{k}=s^{k}:s^{k-1}:\dots:s^{1}:u^{0}_{\gamma}
and with
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(u^{0}_{\alpha})=\alpha
.
A run descriptor
\tau
(having state
q
) describes the suffix
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
.
In
D
we include a derivation tree
D^{\prime}
for
\alpha\vdash\tau
, talking about the parts of
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
for which the new topmost
0
-stack
u^{0}_{\alpha}
is responsible,
because
u^{0}_{\gamma}
is responsible for creating
u^{0}_{\alpha}
.
For
i\in[1,k-1]\cup[k+1,n]
, the assumption set
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
refers to the
i
-stack
s^{i}
,
and hence we take assumptions from
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
to
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
.
The assumption set
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau)
, in turn, refers to the
k
-stack
t^{k}
, not directly to
s^{k}
.
Because of that, we need a composer to decompose the assumption set
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau)
to sets
\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{0}
, referring to stacks
s^{k},s^{k-1},\dots,s^{1},u^{0}_{\gamma}
.
Then, for
i\in[1,k]
, assumptions from
\Phi^{i}
are taken to
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
.
Elements of
\Phi^{0}
refer to our
0
-stack
u^{0}_{\gamma}
, and hence should be realized by our derivation tree.
Thus, for every run descriptor in
\Phi^{0}
we provide a derivation tree (in the set
\mathfrak{D}
).
We should set
\sigma
to be productive if
u^{0}_{\gamma}
is responsible for reading some
\sharp
symbols,
or when some productive run descriptor from an assumption set is used more than once.
Maybe this happens inside
\tau
or inside some run descriptor from
\Phi^{0}
; when any of them is productive, we set
\sigma
to be productive.
But it may also happen that a productive run descriptor is used as an assumption by
\tau
and by some run descriptor from
\Phi^{0}
,
or that a productive run descriptor is used as an assumption by multiple run descriptors from
\Phi^{0}
(in the latter case, the composer is productive);
in both these situation we also set
\sigma
to be productive.

As already said, a derivation tree
D
is not a representation of the whole run
R
, it only talks about parts of
R
for which the topmost
0
-stack
u^{0}_{\gamma}
is responsible.
It is not, however, a precise representation even of these parts.
This can be seen in Case (
4
) of the definition.
It is possible that
\tau
uses some run descriptor
\xi
from the assumption set
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau)
more than once.
This means that we have multiple suffixes of the run
R
described by
\xi
.
But in
\mathfrak{D}
we include only one derivation tree talking about
\xi
, corresponding to one of the suffixes of
R
.
Thus, in a sense, a derivation tree for
\gamma\vdash\sigma
is a proof that a run described by
\sigma
exists, rather than a way of representing any such run.

{exa}

Consider the
2
-DPDA
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
depicted below; its stack alphabet is
\{\gamma\}
, and input alphabet
A=\{a,b,\sharp\}
.

The arrow from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
denotes that
\delta(q_{1},\gamma)=(q_{2},\mathsf{push}^{1}_{\gamma})
, the arrow from
q_{4}
to
q_{5}
denotes that
\delta(q_{4})=\mathsf{read}(\vec{q})
with
\vec{q}(a)=q_{5}
, and so on.
Take also a monoid
M=\{1,\mathsf{ne}\}
with
1\cdot 1=1
and
1\cdot\mathsf{ne}=\mathsf{ne}\cdot 1=\mathsf{ne}\cdot\mathsf{ne}=\mathsf{ne}
,
and a morphism
\varphi:A^{*}\to M
that maps the empty word to
1
and all nonempty words to
\mathsf{ne}
.

Consider run descriptors

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}

\displaystyle=(q_{i},\{(1,(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))\},\emptyset,\mathsf{np})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i\in\{5,6,7\}\,,\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle\sigma_{4,f}

\displaystyle=(q_{4},\{(\mathsf{ne},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))\},\emptyset,f)

\displaystyle\mbox{for }f\in\{\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}\}\,.

They describe runs that are compositions of a
2
-return, and of a run that starts in the state
q_{3}
and reads some
\sharp
symbols (is productive).
For
i\in\{5,6,7\}
, the
2
-return should not read anything.
Thus, in this case
D_{i}=(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,q_{i},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))
is a derivation tree for
\gamma\vdash\sigma_{i}
.
On the other hand, for
i=4
the
2
-return should read a nonempty word, which should contain some
\sharp
symbol when
f=\mathsf{pr}
.
We can derive
\gamma\vdash\sigma_{4,\mathsf{pr}}
by
D_{4,\sharp}=(\mathsf{read},q_{4},D_{7})
, and
\gamma\vdash\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}
by
D_{4,a}=(\mathsf{read},q_{4},D_{5})
, as well as by
D_{4,b}=(\mathsf{read},q_{4},D_{6})
.

For
f\in\{\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}\}
, denote
\sigma_{4,f}^{1}=\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma_{4,f})
, that is,
\sigma_{4,f}^{1}=(q_{4},\{(\mathsf{ne},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))\},f)
.
The next run descriptors that we consider are

\displaystyle\sigma_{3,f}=(q_{3},\{(\mathsf{ne},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))\},\{(1,\sigma_{4,f}^{1})\},\mathsf{np})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }f\in\{\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}\}\,.

They describe runs whose some suffix (that starts before reading anything, and after a
1
-return) is described by
\sigma_{4,f}^{1}
,
and some other suffix (that starts after reading a nonempty word, and after a
2
-return) is described by
(q_{3},\mathsf{pr})
.
We can derive
\gamma\vdash\sigma_{3,f}
by
D_{3,f}=(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,q_{3},\sigma_{4,f}^{1})
, for
f\in\{\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}\}
.
Notice that the derivation trees
D_{3,f}
(and the former ones as well) specify precisely only a prefix of a run;
more precisely, the fragment of the run that corresponds to the topmost
0
-stack, before using an assumption.
It is not checked that the assumption can be fulfilled by any stack that would be placed below the topmost
0
-stack.
We can equally well have a derivation tree like
(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,q_{3},\xi_{4})
with
\xi_{4}=(q_{4},\{(1,(q_{1},\mathsf{pr}))\},\mathsf{pr})
,
which derives
\gamma\vdash(q_{3},\{(1,(q_{1},\mathsf{pr}))\},\{(1,\xi_{4})\},\mathsf{np})
, although it is impossible to deliver
\xi_{4}
by any
1
-stack
(because, e.g., there is no
2
-return that ends in the state
q_{1}
).

Next, for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\{\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}\}
consider run descriptors

\displaystyle\sigma_{2,f_{1},f_{2}}=(q_{2},\{(\mathsf{ne},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))\},\{(1,\sigma_{4,f_{1}}^{1}),(\mathsf{ne},\sigma_{4,f_{2}}^{1})\},f_{3})\,,

\displaystyle\mbox{where }f_{3}=\mathsf{pr}\Leftrightarrow f_{1}=f_{2}=\mathsf{pr}\,.

We can derive
\gamma\vdash\sigma_{2,f_{1},f_{2}}
by
D_{2,f_{1},f_{2}}=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,q_{2},D_{3,f_{1}},\{D_{3,f_{2}}\})
.
The derivation tree has to specify fragments of the run for which the topmost
0
-stack is responsible.
When applied to a stack
s^{2}:s^{1}:s^{0}
, the
\mathsf{push}^{2}_{\gamma}
operation (resulting in
(s^{2}:s^{1}:s^{0}):\mathsf{p}_{+1}(s^{1}:s^{0})
) splits the topmost
0
-stack
s^{0}
into two copies;

D_{2,f_{1},f_{2}}
says that, with the new copy of
s^{0}
, we should continue according to the derivation tree
D_{3,f_{1}}
.
The run descriptor
\mathsf{rd}(D_{3,f_{1}})
(i.e.,
\sigma_{3,f_{1}}
) says that the
1
-stack
s^{1}
will be used with run descriptor
\sigma_{4,f_{1}}^{1}
(before reading anything),
and that the
2
-stack
s^{2}:s^{1}:s^{0}
will be used with run descriptor
(q_{3},\mathsf{pr})
(after reading a nonempty word).
The definition of our derivation tree involves a composer

\displaystyle(\{(\mathsf{ne},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))\},\{(\mathsf{ne},\sigma_{4,f_{2}}^{1})\},\{(\mathsf{ne},\sigma_{3,f_{2}})\};\{(\mathsf{ne},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))\};\mathsf{np})\,.

It says that in order to provide
(q_{3},\mathsf{pr})
by the
2
-stack
s^{2}:s^{1}:s^{0}
,
we have to provide
\sigma_{3,f_{2}}
by
s^{0}
, and
\sigma_{4,f_{2}}^{1}
by
s^{1}
, and
(q_{3},\mathsf{pr})
by
s^{2}
.
Because
(q_{3},\mathsf{pr})
for
s^{2}:s^{1}:s^{0}
will be used after reading a nonempty word (the pair in the output set of the composer is
(\mathsf{ne},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))
),
also the run descriptors for
s^{0},s^{1},s^{2}
will be used after reading a nonempty word.
The derivation tree
D_{2,f_{1},f_{2}}
says also how the lower copy of
s^{0}
provides
\sigma_{3,f_{2}}
; this is described by the derivation tree
D_{3,f_{2}}
.
In the order-
1
assumption set of
\sigma_{2,f_{1},f_{2}}
we put both assumptions,
(1,\sigma_{4,f_{1}}^{1})
and
(\mathsf{ne},\sigma_{4,f_{2}}^{1})
;
the former needs to be provided by the new copy of
s^{1}
, and the latter by the lower copy of
s^{1}
.
Notice that
\sigma_{2,\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{pr}}
is productive, because for
f_{1}=f_{2}=\mathsf{pr}
the same (productive) run descriptor
\sigma_{4,\mathsf{pr}}^{1}
is used for
s^{1}
twice.

Consider also run descriptors

\displaystyle\sigma_{1,f}=(q_{1},\{(\mathsf{ne},(q_{3},\mathsf{pr}))\},\emptyset,f)

\displaystyle\mbox{for }f\in\{\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}\}\,.

We can derive
\gamma\vdash\sigma_{1,\mathsf{np}}
by
D_{1,a,a}=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,q_{1},D_{2,\mathsf{np},\mathsf{np}},\{D_{4,a}\})
.
After performing
\mathsf{push}^{1}_{\gamma}
from
s^{2}:s^{1}:s^{0}
, we obtain
s^{2}:(s^{1}:s^{0}):\mathsf{p}_{+1}(s^{0})
.
The derivation tree
D_{2,\mathsf{np},\mathsf{np}}
specifies the behavior of the new topmost
0
-stack.
The composer

\displaystyle(\emptyset,\{(1,\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}),(\mathsf{ne},\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}})\};\{(1,\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}^{1}),(\mathsf{ne},\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}^{1})\};\mathsf{np})

specifies how the assumptions of
\mathsf{rd}(D_{2,\mathsf{np},\mathsf{np}})
(i.e.,
\sigma_{2,\mathsf{np},\mathsf{np}}
) can be realized by the
1
-stack
s^{1}:s^{0}
.
It says that (the lower copy of)
s^{0}
should provide
\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}
, and the derivation tree
D_{4,a}
specifies how it is provided.
The whole
D_{1,a,a}
corresponds to a run that reads the letter
a
twice.
Similarly,
\gamma\vdash\sigma_{1,\mathsf{np}}
can be derived by
D_{1,b,b}=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,q_{1},D_{2,\mathsf{np},\mathsf{np}},\{D_{4,b}\})
,
which corresponds to a run that reads the letter
b
twice.
On the other hand, there is no derivation tree corresponding to a run that first reads the letter
a
, and then the letter
b
(or vice versa).
The reason is that we have to provide exactly one realization of
\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}
for
s^{0}
; this can be either
D_{4,a}
, or
D_{4,b}
, but not both.
The statement
\gamma\vdash\sigma_{1,\mathsf{pr}}
can be derived by
D_{1,\sharp,\sharp}=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,q_{1},D_{2,\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{pr}},\{D_{4,\sharp}\})
,
by
D_{1,\sharp,a}=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,q_{1},D_{2,\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{np}},\{D_{4,\sharp},D_{4,a}\})
,
by
D_{1,a,\sharp}=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,q_{1},D_{2,\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}},\{D_{4,\sharp},D_{4,a}\})
,
and by similar derivation trees using the letter
b
instead of
a
.
Notice that here we have sets with two derivation trees,
D_{4,\sharp}
and
D_{4,a}
;
this is because one provides a realization for
\sigma_{4,\mathsf{pr}}
, and the other for
\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}
.

Finally, consider run descriptors

\displaystyle\tau_{7}

\displaystyle=(q_{7},\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathsf{np})\,,

\displaystyle\tau_{4}

\displaystyle=(q_{4},\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathsf{pr})\,,

and

\displaystyle\tau_{3}

\displaystyle=(q_{3},\emptyset,\{(1,\tau_{4}^{1})\},\mathsf{np})

\displaystyle\mbox{with }\tau_{4}^{1}=\mathsf{red}^{1}(\tau_{4})=(q_{4},\emptyset,\mathsf{pr})\,.

Run descriptors
\tau_{4}
and
\tau_{7}
have empty assumption sets, hence they describe runs that never uncover stacks that are below the topmost
0
-stack
(in other words, these are runs whose every prefix is
0
-upper).
We can derive
\gamma\vdash\tau_{7}
by
E_{7}=(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,q_{7})
, which corresponds to the empty run;

\gamma\vdash\tau_{4}
by
E_{4}=(\mathsf{read},q_{4},E_{7})
, which corresponds to the run that reads
\sharp
and stops;
and
\gamma\vdash\tau_{3}
by
E_{3}=(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,q_{3},\tau_{4}^{1})
.

Annotated Stacks

A derivation tree provides an information about a part of a run
R
for which a particular
0
-stack is responsible.
In order to describe the whole
R
, we have to specify derivation trees for all
0
-stacks in
R(0)
.
To the end, we annotate stacks using sets of derivation trees.

An
annotated
k
-stack
is a positionless
3
3

3

It turns out that while considering annotated
k
-stacks we do not need positions,
so for notational convenience we assume that annotated stacks are positionless (i.e., their
0
-stacks do not contain positions, conversely to non-annotated stacks).

k
-stack over an extended alphabet, whose elements are pairs
(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
, where
\gamma\in\Gamma

and
\mathfrak{D}
is a set of derivation trees having conclusions with the stack symbol
\gamma
, and different run descriptors
(that is,
\mathsf{rd}(D)=\mathsf{rd}(E)
for
D,E\in\mathfrak{D}
implies
D=E
).
Annotated stacks are denoted using boldface letters, often with their order written in the superscript:
\mathbf{s}^{0}
,
\mathbf{t}^{5}
, etc.
The projection of each letter in an annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{s}^{k}
to the
\Gamma
coordinate is denoted by
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{k})
.

We also define the type of an annotated
k
-stack, which is a subset of
{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))=\{\mathsf{rd}(D)\colon\,D\in\mathfrak{D}\}\,,\qquad\qquad\mathsf{type}([\,])=\emptyset\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})\}\,.

We always want to annotate stacks in a consistent way.
Intuitively, when a run descriptor assigned to some stack element requires some assumptions,
then the part of the stack that is below has to deliver annotations fulfilling these assumptions.
Simultaneously, all annotations have to be useful:
they cannot provide derivation trees for run descriptors that do not appear as assumptions of annotations assigned higher in the stack.
To formalize this, we define below when an annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{s}^{k}
is
well-formed
.

{defi}

Each annotated
0
-stack, and the empty annotated
k
-stack for each
k\geq 1
, are always well-formed.
An annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
is well-formed if both
\mathbf{s}^{k}
and
\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
are well-formed,
and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})\}
, and
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})|
.

In the sequel, generally we only consider well-formed annotated stacks (except for some moments when we first define an annotated stack, and then we prove that it is well-formed).

Notice that beside of the condition mentioned earlier (saying that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})
provides exactly assumptions for run descriptors in
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
),
we also have the second condition,
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})|
,
saying that every run descriptor in
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
has exactly one realization by a composition of run descriptors from
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})
and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
.
Both conditions have the same goal, which is also the same as for analogous conditions in the definition of a composer (Definition
7.1
):
we want to estimate the number of
\sharp
symbols read by a run by looking at the number of productive run descriptors associated to
0
-stacks in an annotated stacks.
Two realizations of the same run descriptor, as well as realizations of a run descriptor not appearing in an assumption set, would bias these calculations.

The definition of types and well-formedness connects only the type of
\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
with the types of
\mathbf{s}^{k}
and
\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
,
but similar conditions can be written for a stack of the form
\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
.

Proposition 15
.

Let
0\leq l\leq k\leq n
, and let
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
be an annotated
k
-stack in which each
\mathbf{s}^{i}
is well-formed.
Then,

(T1)

\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}
.

Moreover,
\mathbf{s}
is well-formed if and only if

(T2)

\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}
for every
i\in[l+1,k]
,

(T3)

|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|
.

Proof 7.2
.

Induction on
k-l
.
Suppose first that
k-l=0
.
In this case, Item
(T1)
holds because
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{l}
and because
\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)=\sigma
when
\sigma\in{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
.
Moreover
\mathbf{s}
is well-formed by assumption, Item
(T2)
is true because
[l+1,k]=\emptyset
is empty,
and Item
(T3)
is true because
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{l}
; thus we have the equivalence.

Suppose now that
k-l\geq 1
, and denote
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\mathbf{s}^{k-2}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
.
We then have
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{t}
.
We apply the induction assumption to
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\mathbf{s}^{k-2}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
.
By Item
(T1)
of the induction assumption,
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k-1}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}
,
and by the definition of the type of
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{t}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})\}=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\mathsf{red}^{k-1}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}\,.

Recalling that
\mathsf{red}^{k}(\mathsf{red}^{k-1}(\sigma))=\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)
, we obtain Item
(T1)
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{s}
is well-formed.
Then, in particular,
\mathbf{t}
is well-formed, so Items
(T2)
and
(T3)
hold for the substack
\mathbf{t}
by the induction assumption.
Item
(T2)
from the induction assumption is the same as our Item
(T2)
for
i\in[l+1,k-1]
.
By well-formedness of
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{t}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})\}=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\mathsf{red}^{k-1}(\sigma)))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}\,.

Recalling that
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\mathsf{red}^{k-1}(\sigma))=\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)
we obtain Item
(T2)
for
i=k
.
Moreover, Item
(T3)
of the induction assumption says that
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|
, and by well-formedness of
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{t}
we have that
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})|
,
thus
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|
(Item
(T3)
).

Conversely, suppose that Items
(T2)
and
(T3)
hold for
\mathbf{s}
.
Because
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})
is the image of
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})
under the function
\mathsf{red}^{k}
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})
is the image of
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})
under the function
\mathsf{red}^{k-1}
,
we necessarily have
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|\leq|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})|\leq|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|
, and thus
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|
(Item
(T3)
)
implies that
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|
; we thus have Item
(T3)
for
\mathbf{t}
.
Moreover, Item
(T2)
for
\mathbf{t}
is a direct consequence of this item for
\mathbf{s}
(we only restrict the considered orders
i
to
[l+1,k-1]
).
Thus, by the induction assumption,
\mathbf{t}
is well-formed.
Using Item
(T2)
for
i=k
, the equality
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)=\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\mathsf{red}^{k-1}(\sigma))
, and Item
(T1)
for
\mathbf{t}
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\mathsf{red}^{k-1}(\sigma)))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})\}\,.

Together with the equality
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})|
this implies that
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{t}
is well-formed.

An annotated stack
\mathbf{s}
is called
singular
if
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=1
.
When an annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}^{n}
is singular, we define
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}^{n})
to be the configuration
(q,\mathsf{pos}_{+}(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{n})))
,
where
q
is the state of the only run descriptor in
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{n})
.

As the type of a configuration
c
, denoted
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(c)
, we take the union of
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{n}))
over all well-formed singular annotated
n
-stacks
\mathbf{s}^{n}
such that
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}^{n})=c
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(c)=\bigcup\{\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{n}))\colon\,\mathbf{s}^{n}\mbox{ well-formed, }\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}^{n})=c\}\,.

We remark that in the union we could also allow well-formed annotated stacks
\mathbf{s}^{n}
that are not necessarily singular,
but are such that
\mathsf{pos}_{+}(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{n}))
is the stack of
c
and the state of all run descriptors in
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{n})
is the state of
c
.
On the other hand, in general there does not exist a single well-formed annotated stack
\mathbf{s}^{n}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{n}))=\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(c)
. Namely, we can have a situation like in Example
7.1
, where we cannot assign both
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{1}^{\prime}
to the topmost
0
-stack,
as both of them result in the same run descriptor
\xi_{1}
for the topmost
1
-stack.

Actually, we see a direct connection between the well-formedness property and composers.

Proposition 16
.

Let
0\leq l\leq k\leq n
, let
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
be an annotated
k
-stack in which each
\mathbf{s}^{i}
is well-formed, and let
\Psi^{k}\subseteq M\times{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
.
The following two conditions are equivalent:

•

there exists a composer
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{l};\Psi^{k};f)
such that
\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})
for each
i\in[l,k]
, and

•

\mathbf{s}
is well-formed and
\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})
.

Proof 7.3
.

Suppose first that we have a composer
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{l};\Psi^{k};f)
such that

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i\in[k,l]\,.

(4)

Notice that

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})\}=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})\,,

(5)

where the consecutive equalities follow from Item
(T1)
of Proposition
15
, from Equality (
4
),
and from Condition
(C2)
of Definition
7.1
, respectively.
Moreover, for
i\in[l+1,k]
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})\}=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}\,,

(6)

where the equalities are consequences of Equality (
4
), of Condition
(C1)
of Definition
7.1
, and of Equality (
4
) again.
Simultaneously,

\displaystyle|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})|=|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|\,,

(7)

where the consecutive equalities follow from Equality (
5
), from Condition
(C3)
of Definition
7.1
, and from Equality (
4
), respectively.
Equalities (
6
) and (
7
) give Items
(T2)
and
(T3)
of Proposition
15
,
which implies that
\mathbf{s}
is well-formed; together with Equality (
5
) this gives the thesis.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathbf{s}
is well-formed and

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\,.

(8)

We define

\displaystyle\Phi^{l}

\displaystyle=\{(m,\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\land(m,\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma))\in\Psi^{k}\}\,,

and

(9)

\displaystyle\Phi^{i}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{m\circ\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)\colon\,(m,\sigma)\in\Phi^{l}\}\qquad\mbox{for }i\in[l+1,k]\,.

(10)

By Equality (
8
) and by Item
(T1)
of Proposition
15
we have that

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}\,.

(11)

This means that for every
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})
there is some
m
such that
(m,\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma))\in\Psi^{k}
.
In the light of Equality (
9
) this implies that

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\,.

(12)

On the other hand, by Equality (
10
) and by Item
(T2)
of Proposition
15
, for
i\in[l+1,k]
, we have that

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})\}=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\,.

(13)

Let us now check particular conditions of Definition
7.1
.
Condition
(C1)
is immediate from Equality (
10
).
By Equality (
9
), for every pair
(m,\sigma)\in\Phi^{l}
, the pair
(m,\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma))
is in
\Psi^{k}
.
Conversely, by Equality (
11
), every pair in
\Psi^{k}
is of the form
(m,\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma))
with
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})
, hence
(m,\sigma)\in\Phi^{l}
by Equality (
9
).
This implies Condition
(C2)
.
Using consecutively Equality (
8
), Item
(T3)
of Proposition
15
, and Equality (
12
), we obtain Condition
(C3)
:

\displaystyle|\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|=|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})|\,.

Condition
(C4)
always holds for some
f\in\{\mathsf{np},\mathsf{pr}\}
.
Thus
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{l};\Psi^{k};f)
is a composer, which together with Equalities (
12
) and (
13
) gives the thesis.

{exa}

This is a continuation of Example
7.1
.
With the derivation trees considered there,

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{1}

\displaystyle=[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{1,\sharp,a}\})]]

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{2}

\displaystyle=[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,\sharp},D_{4,a}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{2,\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{np}}\})]]

are well-formed (singular) annotated
2
-stacks.
On the other hand, the following annotated
2
-stacks are not well-formed:

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{3}

\displaystyle=[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\{E_{1}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{1,\sharp,a}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{4}

\displaystyle=[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,\sharp},D_{4,a},D_{4,b}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{2,\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{np}}\})]]\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{5}

\displaystyle=[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,\sharp}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{2,\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{np}}\})]]\,.

In
\mathbf{s}_{3}
, we provide a spare derivation tree
E_{1}
, not needed by the derivation trees assigned above.
In
\mathbf{s}_{4}
, two derivation trees,
D_{4,a}
and
D_{4,b}
, provide the same run descriptor.
Finally, in
\mathbf{s}_{5}
, we are missing a derivation tree that would provide
\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}
.

When
\widetilde{\Psi}
is a subset of the type of a well-formed annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{s}
,
we can remove some of the annotations in
\mathbf{s}
in order to obtain a well-formed annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\widetilde{\Psi}}
whose type is
\widetilde{\Psi}
.
We do this by induction:

•

For
k=0
, we restrict the set of derivation trees in
\mathbf{s}
to those trees whose run descriptor is in
\widetilde{\Psi}
.

•

The type of the empty stack is empty, so we need not to restrict it in any way.

•

For
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
, we restrict
\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
to the set
\widetilde{\Phi}
containing those
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})

for which
\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\in\widetilde{\Psi}
, and we restrict
\mathbf{s}^{k}
to
\bigcup_{\sigma\in\widetilde{\Phi}}\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))
.

Plan for the Remaining Part of the Section

We have already defined types of configurations, as needed for Theorem
11
.
Type of a configuration
c
is defined via existence of annotated stacks
\mathbf{s}
such that
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})=c
.
Theorem
11
says that if two configurations
c,d
have the same type, and we have a run starting in
c
, then a similar run starts
d
.
Roughly, the strategy of the proof is as follows.
First, basing on the run starting in
c
, we construct an annotated stack
\mathbf{s}
with
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})=c
, corresponding to this run.
More precisely, we do not process the whole run in this way, but rather its particular fragments.
Then, because the types of
c
and
d
equal, there exists an annotated stack
\mathbf{t}
with
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})=d
, and with
\mathsf{rd}(\mathbf{t})=\mathsf{rd}(\mathbf{s})
.
Having
\mathbf{t}
we proceed in the opposite direction: basing on the annotated stack
\mathbf{t}
we construct a run starting in
d
, satisfying the thesis of the theorem.

Recall that an annotated stack is, roughly, a description of a run.
The run described by an annotated stack is called
annotated run
, and is defined in Subsection
7.2
.
In Lemma
27
(located in Subsection
7.4
) we prove that annotated runs have the expected form,
that is, that using an assumption of a run descriptor corresponds to performing a return.
In Subsection
7.5
we present the opposite direction: how to construct an annotated stack basing on a run.
The proof of Theorem
11
is finalized in Subsection
7.6
.

Simultaneously, we prepare ourselves for a proof of Theorem
12
, which additionally talks about the number of
\sharp
symbols read by a run.
We thus need to estimate the number of
\sharp
symbols read by an annotated run.
To this end, in Subsection
7.2
we define two numbers corresponding to an annotated stack
\mathbf{s}
, namely
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})
.
They provide a lower bound and, respectively, an upper bound for the number of
\sharp
symbols read by an annotated run starting in
\mathbf{s}
, as showed in Lemma
18
.
(We also define there a third number,
\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})
, and we prove that it gives an upper bound for the length of an annotated run starting in
\mathbf{s}
.
Its role is auxiliary: it is only needed for showing that the constructed annotated run is finite.
Actually, this is needed already while proving Theorem
11
.)
The essential property is that
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})
can be bounded by a function of
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
, as shown in Proposition
26
, contained in Subsection
7.3
.
Thus
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
itself estimates the number of
\sharp
symbols read by an annotated run starting in
\mathbf{s}
.
With such a function
\mathsf{low}
we can define sequence-equivalence, as needed in Theorem
12
.
Namely, having a sequence of configurations (all of the same type), for every run descriptor
\sigma
it is enough to know one think:
whether there is a sequence of annotated stacks corresponding to these configurations (and to the run descriptor
\sigma
),
and such that the values of
\mathsf{low}
for these annotated stacks are bounded.
If this is the case, using these annotated stacks we can reproduce runs that read a bounded number of
\sharp
symbol.
If not, then a sequence of annotated stacks corresponding to the considered configurations also exists, but the values of
\mathsf{low}
for these annotated stacks are unbounded,
and in effect the reproduced runs read an unbounded number of
\sharp
symbols.
A proof of Theorem
12
, following these ideas, is given in Subsection
7.7
.

7.2.
Annotated Runs

In this subsection we describe how, having an annotated stack, we can reproduce a run.
This is formalized in the notion of annotated runs.
We also relate the number of
\sharp
symbols read by a run with the number of productive run descriptors in annotations of the starting configuration.
Later, in Subsection
7.5
, we do the converse: we show how to construct an annotated stack basing on a run.
Recall that when a well-formed annotated stack is singular, then its topmost
0
-stack is singular as well (cf. Item
(T3)
of Proposition
15
).

{defi}

Let
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{0}
be a well-formed singular annotated
n
-stack, where
\mathbf{s}^{0}=(\gamma,\{D\})
.
We define the
successor
of
\mathbf{s}
.

(1)

If
D=(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,p)
, then
\mathbf{s}
has no successor.

(2)

If
D=(\mathsf{read},p,D^{\prime})
, then the successor is
\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}:(\gamma,\{D^{\prime}\})
.

(3)

If
D=(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,p,\tau^{k})
, then the successor is
\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k}
.

(4)

Suppose that
D=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,p,D^{\prime},\mathfrak{D})
.
Let
\alpha
,
k
,
\Psi^{i}
,
\Phi^{i}
be as in Definition
7.1
(
4
).
In this situation, the successor of
\mathbf{s}
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{t}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k-1})}:\mathbf{s}^{k-2}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k-2})}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{1})}:(\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\})\,,

where
\mathbf{t}^{k}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k})}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k-1})}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{1})}:(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
.

We notice that in Case (
4
) for
i\in[1,k]
we have that
\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})\subseteq\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})
,
and for
i\in[1,k-1]
we have that
\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\subseteq\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})
, and thus the restrictions are legal.
Indeed, from Proposition
15
applied to
\mathbf{s}
we know that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D)))
for all
i\in[1,n]
;
moreover, by Definition
7.1
(
4
),
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)=\Psi^{i}\cup\Phi^{i}
for
i\in[1,k-1]
, and
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)=\Phi^{k}
.

Proposition 17
.

Let
\mathbf{t}
be
the successor of a well-formed singular annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}
.
Then
\mathbf{t}
is singular, well-formed, and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
is a successor of
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
(in the considered automaton).

Proof 7.4
.

Let
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{0}
, and
\mathbf{s}^{0}=(\gamma,\{D\})
, and
\sigma=\mathsf{rd}(D)
.
All
\mathbf{s}^{i}
are well-formed, because
\mathbf{s}
is well-formed.
Moreover, by Proposition
15
,
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))
for all
i\in[1,n]
.

We have several cases depending on the shape of
\mathbf{s}
.
We cannot have
D=(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,p)
, as then
\mathbf{s}
has no successor.

Suppose that
D=(\mathsf{read},p,D^{\prime})
.
Recall from Definition
7.1
that
\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime}))=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))
for all
i\in[1,n]
.
By Proposition
15
,
\mathbf{t}
is singular (its
\mathsf{type}
is a singleton
\{\mathsf{red}^{n}(\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime}))\}
) and well-formed.
Moreover,
\delta(\gamma,p)=\mathsf{read}(\vec{q})
, where
\vec{q}(a)
equals the state of
\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})
for some
a\in A
.
The transition from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
reading this
a
leads to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
.

Next, suppose that
D=(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,p,\tau^{k})
.
By Definition
7.1
we have that
\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))=\{\tau^{k}\}
(hence
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\{\tau^{k}\}
)
and
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau^{k})
(hence
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau^{k}))
) for
i\in[k+1,n]
.
Proposition
15
applied to
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k}
implies that it is singular and well-formed.
Moreover,
\delta(\gamma,p)=(q,\mathsf{pop}^{k})
, where
q
is the state of
\tau^{k}
, so the transition from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
leads to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
.

Finally, suppose that
D=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,p,D^{\prime},\mathfrak{D})
.
Let
\alpha
,
k
,
\Psi^{i}
,
\Phi^{i}
,
f
be as in Definition
7.1
(
4
), and
\mathbf{t}^{k}
as in Definition
7.2
(
4
).
Obviously,
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})})=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})
, for all
i\in[1,k]
;
moreover,
\mathsf{type}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))=\{\mathsf{rd}(E)\colon\,E\in\mathfrak{D}\}=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
, by Definition
7.1
(
4
).
Furthermore, again by this definition,
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{0};\Psi^{k};f)
is a composer.
It follows from Proposition
16
that
\mathbf{t}^{k}
is well-formed, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}^{k})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})

(recall that
\mathbf{t}^{k}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k})}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k-1})}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{1})}:(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
).
By Definition
7.1
(
4
), for all
i\in[1,n]
we have that
\Psi^{i}=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime}))
.
Moreover,

•

for
i\in[k+1,n]
, we have
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)=\Psi^{i}
by Definition
7.1
(
4
), so

\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})))
;

•

\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}^{k})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime}))
;

•

for
i\in[1,k-1]
, we have that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime}))
.

In effect,
\mathbf{t}
, which is a composition of these stacks, and of
(\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\})
is singular and well-formed by Proposition
15
.
Additionally,
\delta(\gamma,p)=(q,\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha})
, where
q
is the state of
\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})
, so the transition from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
leads to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
.

An
annotated run

\mathbf{R}
is a sequence
\mathbf{s}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{s}_{m}
of well-formed singular
n
-stacks in which
\mathbf{s}_{i}
is the successor of
\mathbf{s}_{i-1}
for each
i\in[1,m]
.
By replacing each
\mathbf{s}_{i}
by
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}_{i})
we obtain a run denoted
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R})
.

Notice that an annotated stack
\mathbf{s}
may have less successors than
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
.
Indeed, in the case of
D=(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,p)
there are no successors of
\mathbf{s}
, but
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
may have successors.
Similarly, in the case of
D=(\mathsf{read},p,D^{\prime})
there is exactly one successor of
\mathbf{s}
(the state in
\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})
determines which letter should be read),
while in a run from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
we can read any letter.

{exa}

Recall the
2
-DPDA
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
from Example
7.1
,
and the annotated stack
\mathbf{s}_{1}=[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{1,\sharp,a}\})]]
from Example
7.1
.
The successors of
\mathbf{s}_{1}
are, consecutively,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,\sharp},D_{4,a}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{2,\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{np}}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,a}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{3,\mathsf{np}}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,\sharp}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{3,\mathsf{pr}}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,a}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{3,\mathsf{np}}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,\sharp}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,a}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{3,\mathsf{np}}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{7}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,a}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{3,\mathsf{np}}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{4,a}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})],[(\gamma,\emptyset),(\gamma,\{D_{5}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{4}\})]]\,,

\displaystyle[[(\gamma,\{E_{7}\})]]\,;

the last of them has no more successors.
In the transition between the first and the second line,
D_{2,\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{np}}
says that the new topmost
0
-stack should be annotated by
D_{3,\mathsf{pr}}
,
and that the previously topmost
0
-stack should be annotated by
D_{3,\mathsf{np}}
.
Because
\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\mathsf{rd}(D_{3,\mathsf{pr}}))=\sigma_{4,\mathsf{pr}}^{1}
and
\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\mathsf{rd}(D_{3,\mathsf{np}}))=\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}^{1}
,
we know that
D_{4,\sharp}
should be taken to the topmost
1
-stack (we have that
\mathsf{red}^{1}(\mathsf{rd}(D_{4,\sharp}))=\sigma_{4,\mathsf{pr}}^{1}
),
and that
D_{4,a}
should be left in the second topmost
1
-stack (we have that
\mathsf{red}^{1}(\mathsf{rd}(D_{4,a}))=\sigma_{4,\mathsf{np}}^{1}
).

We can see that not every run is of the form
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R})
for some annotated run
\mathbf{R}
.
For example, this is the case for the run that starts in
(q_{1},\mathsf{pos}_{+}([[\gamma,\gamma],[\gamma,\gamma]]))
and reads
a
, then
b
, and then
\sharp
.
Indeed, in order to obtain such a run as an annotated run, to the topmost
0
-stack we have to assign a hypothetical derivation tree
D_{1,a,b}
, saying that we should first read
a
and then
b
,
but there is no such derivation tree (as already explained in Example
7.1
).
Another run that is not of the form
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R})
for any annotated run
\mathbf{R}
is the run that starts in
(q_{2},\mathsf{pos}_{+}([[\gamma,\gamma],[\gamma,\gamma,\gamma]]))
and reads
a
, then
b
, and then
\sharp
.
This time the problem is that to the second topmost
0
-stack we cannot assign simultaneously
D_{4,a}
and
D_{4,b}
, as they both have the same run descriptor.
More generally, the
\mathsf{push}
in Case (
4
) of Definition
7.2
leaves the same annotations in the original substack as in the copied substack, up to a restriction,
which causes that fragments of the annotated run corresponding to these substacks have to be the same.

A priori there might exist an infinite annotated run.
But, as we see below, this is impossible:
always after some number of steps we reach an annotated stack with no successors (Case (
1
) of Definition
7.2
).
Moreover, we show that the number of
\sharp
symbols read by the run starting in an annotated stack
\mathbf{s}
can be estimated by the number of productive run descriptors in the annotations of
\mathbf{s}
.
To this end, to each well-formed annotated stack
\mathbf{s}
we assign three natural numbers:
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
,
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})
, and
\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})
.
The first two of them give a lower and an upper bound on the number of
\sharp
symbols read by our run, and the last one gives an upper bound on the length of the run.

{defi}

For positive integers
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}
we define
\mathsf{pow}(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})
by induction on
k
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}()=1\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(m_{1},m_{2},\dots,m_{k})=(1+m_{1})^{\mathsf{pow}(m_{2},\dots,m_{k})}-1\,.

Notice that, in particular,
\mathsf{pow}(m_{1})=m_{1}
and
\mathsf{pow}(m_{1},m_{2})=(1+m_{1})^{m_{2}}-1
.

{defi}

For a well-formed annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{s}
we define natural numbers
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
,
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})
, and
\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})
by induction on the structure of
\mathbf{s}
.

•

If
\mathbf{s}=(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
, we take

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}|\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{D\in\mathfrak{D}\colon\,\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}}C_{0pt(D)}\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{D\in\mathfrak{D}}C_{0pt(D)}\,,

where
C_{z}
is defined inductively:

\displaystyle C_{0}=2\,,

and

\displaystyle C_{z+1}=(2|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|)^{n}\cdot(C_{z})^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|+1}\,.

•

We take
\mathsf{low}([\,])=0
and
\mathsf{high}([\,])=\mathsf{len}([\,])=1
.

•

If
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
, we take

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\big(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)\,.

The three numbers are interesting for us, because of the following lemma.
Recall that, for a run
R
, by
\sharp(R)
we denote the number of
\sharp
symbols read by
R
.

Lemma 18
.

If
\mathbf{R}
is an annotated run,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))\leq\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(|\mathbf{R}|))\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))\geq\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(|\mathbf{R}|))\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{R}(0))\geq|\mathbf{R}|+\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{R}(|\mathbf{R}|))\,.

This is one of key lemmas of this section.
We now give some examples and intuitions staying behind this lemma, and behind the definitions of
\mathsf{low}
,
\mathsf{high}
, and
\mathsf{len}
;
after that, we prove this lemma.

We see that the last inequality of Lemma
18
bounds the length of an annotated run
\mathbf{R}
by
\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{R}(0))
,
that is, by a function of the annotated stack
\mathbf{R}(0)
, in which the annotated run starts.
Similarly, the second inequality bounds the number of
\sharp
symbols read by
\mathbf{R}
, by another function of
\mathbf{R}(0)
, namely by
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))
.
The additional components added on the right of these inequalities only strengthen them.
Conversely, the role of the first inequality is to give a lower bound for the number of
\sharp
symbols read by
\mathbf{R}
.
If
\mathbf{R}
is maximal (i.e., cannot be prolonged),
then the topmost
0
-stack of
\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{R})
is annotated by a derivation tree of the form
(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,q)
, and all other
0
-stacks are annotated by empty sets.
In effect
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(|\mathbf{R}|))=0
, and we simply obtain that
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))\leq\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))
.
But for an arbitrary run
\mathbf{R}
, which may end prematurely, even before reading any
\sharp
symbol, we have to add
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(|\mathbf{R}|))
on the right side of the inequality.

Roughly speaking,
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))
counts the number of productive run descriptors in the annotations of
\mathbf{R}(0)
.
The intuition is that every productive run descriptor is responsible for increasing the number of
\sharp
symbols read,
so the first inequality of Lemma
18
should hold with such a definition of
\mathsf{low}
.

We see that the function
\mathsf{high}
takes into account the same productive run descriptors as
\mathsf{low}
,
but instead of sums we use products and the
\mathsf{pow}
function.
Indeed, it is shown in Proposition
21
that if all run descriptors in the annotations of a substack
\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
are nonproductive, then
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})=1
(and
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})=0
).
Suppose that
\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
is singular, that is, its type is a singleton
\{\sigma\}
.
If
\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
is well-formed, we have
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))
.
Observe that
\mathsf{pow}(x,1)=(1+x)^{1}-1=x
for every
x
, and thus

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}))=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),1)=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k})\,.

This is similar to the behavior of the
\mathsf{low}
function, as in such a case we also have

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})+0=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})\,.

Technical details of the definition of
\mathsf{high}
were chosen so that it is possible to perform a proof, but two facts are important here.
First, nonproductive run descriptors cannot be responsible for increasing the number of
\sharp
symbols read,
and thus (in order to obtain the second inequality of Lemma
18
)
it is enough to have a function
\mathsf{high}
that takes into account only productive run descriptors (i.e., ignores nonproductive run descriptors).
Second, because
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))
and
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))
are counting the same productive run descriptors, only in a different way, the two values are related.
Namely,
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))
can be bounded by a function of
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))
.
This essential property is shown in Proposition
26
, in the next subsection.

The function
\mathsf{len}
is defined very similarly to
\mathsf{high}
, but it takes into account all run descriptors, not only productive ones.
Thus, roughly, it depends on the size of the annotated stack.

{exa}

We continue the previous examples, concerning the
2
-DPDA
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
from Example
7.1
.
Consider the annotated stack
\mathbf{s}_{1}=[[\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}],[\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}]]
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{1}=(\gamma,\{D_{1,\sharp,a}\})\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{3}=(\gamma,\{E_{3}\})\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{2}=(\gamma,\emptyset)\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{4}=(\gamma,\{E_{4}\})\,.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{1})=\{\mathsf{rd}(D_{1,\sharp,a})\}=\{\sigma_{1,\mathsf{pr}}\}\,,

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\sigma_{1,\mathsf{pr}}))=\emptyset\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{2})=\emptyset\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{3})=\{\mathsf{rd}(E_{3})\}=\{\tau_{3}\}\,,

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\tau_{3}))=\{\tau_{4}^{1}\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{4})=\{\mathsf{rd}(E_{4})\}=\{\tau_{4}\}\,.

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\tau_{4}))=\emptyset\,.

In effect

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}])=\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{1})\}=\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma_{1,\mathsf{pr}})\}\,,

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{2}(\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma_{1,\mathsf{pr}})))=\{(q_{3},\mathsf{pr})\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}([\mathbf{t}_{2}])=\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{2})\}=\emptyset\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}])=\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{3})\}=\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\tau_{3})\}\,,

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{2}(\mathsf{red}^{1}(\tau_{3})))=\emptyset\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}([\mathbf{t}_{4}])=\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{4})\}=\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\tau_{4})\}=\{\tau_{4}^{1}\}\,,\qquad\mbox{and finally}\hskip-200.0003pt

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}([[\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]])=\{\mathsf{red}^{2}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}])\}=\{\mathsf{red}^{2}(\mathsf{red}^{1}(\tau_{3}))\}=\{(q_{3},\mathsf{pr})\}\,.\hskip-200.0003pt

Recall that
\tau_{3}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
and
\sigma_{1,\mathsf{pr}},\tau_{4}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
.
We can compute
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}_{1})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{4}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{4})}\big(\mathsf{low}([\,]{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{4}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}([\,]{\restriction}_{\emptyset})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{4}{\restriction}_{\{\tau_{4}\}})=\mathsf{low}([\,])+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{4})=0+1=1\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}([[\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}])}\big(\mathsf{low}([\,]{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{2}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}([\,]{\restriction}_{\emptyset})+\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\tau_{3})\}})=0+\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{3})}\big(\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{4}]{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{3}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{4}]{\restriction}_{\{\tau_{4}^{1}\}})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{3}{\restriction}_{\{\tau_{3}\}})=\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{4}])+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{3})=1+0=1\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{2}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{2})}\big(\mathsf{low}([\,]{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{2}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)=0\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{1})}\big(\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{2}]{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{2}]{\restriction}_{\emptyset})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma_{1,\mathsf{pr}}\}})=\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{2}])+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{1})=0+1=1\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}])}\big(\mathsf{low}([[\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]]{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{2}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}]{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}([[\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]]{\restriction}_{\{(q_{3},\mathsf{pr})\}})+\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}]{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{1}(\sigma_{1,\mathsf{pr}})\}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}([[\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]])+\mathsf{low}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}])=1+1=2\,.

We see that the restrictions of annotated stacks appearing in the above formulas do not modify these annotated stacks (this is the case because all annotations are either singletons or empty sets).

Next, we compute
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}_{1})
.
To this end, recall that
0pt(D_{1,\sharp,a})=2
and
0pt(E_{4})=1
.
This time we give the formulas ignoring the restrictions, as again they do not change anything:

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}([\mathbf{t}_{4}]),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}_{3}))=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}([\,]),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}_{4})),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}_{3}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(1,C_{0pt(E_{4})}),1)=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(1,C_{1}),1)=2^{C_{1}}-1\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}([\mathbf{t}_{2}]),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}_{1}))=\mathsf{pow}(1,C_{0}pt(D_{1,\sharp,a}))=2^{C_{2}}-1\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}([[\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]]),\mathsf{high}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}]))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}([\,]),\mathsf{high}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}])),\mathsf{high}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}]))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(1,2^{C_{1}}-1),2^{C_{2}}-1)=2^{(2^{C_{1}}-1)(2^{C_{2}}-1)}-1\,.

Notice that
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}_{3})=1
, because
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}_{3})
contains only a nonproductive run descriptor.

Similarly, we can compute
\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}_{1})
, but this should also take into account
E_{3}
, whose depth is
0
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{len}([\mathbf{t}_{4}]),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{t}_{3}))=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{len}([\,]),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{t}_{4})),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{t}_{3}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(1,C_{0pt(E_{4})}),C_{0pt(E_{3})})=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(1,C_{1}),C_{0})=2^{C_{1}\cdot C_{0}}-1\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{len}([\mathbf{t}_{2}]),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{t}_{1}))=\mathsf{pow}(1,C_{0}pt(D_{1,\sharp,a}))=2^{C_{2}}-1\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{len}([[\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}]]),\mathsf{len}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}]))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{len}([\,]),\mathsf{len}([\mathbf{t}_{4},\mathbf{t}_{3}])),\mathsf{len}([\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{1}]))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(1,2^{C_{1}\cdot C_{0}}-1),2^{C_{2}}-1)=2^{(2^{C_{1}\cdot C_{0}}-1)(2^{C_{2}}-1)}-1\,.

We have said that
\mathsf{low}
counts the number of productive run descriptors in all annotations.
This is a good high-level intuition, but strictly speaking this is not true.
Indeed, say that we have two run descriptors
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
such that
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)=\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma^{\prime})
.
Then, in the formula for
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
we add
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})
twice (once for
\sigma
, and once for
\sigma^{\prime}
).
This is illustrated by the next example.

{exa}

Consider the
2
-DPDA
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
depicted below; its stack alphabet is
\{\gamma\}
, and input alphabet
\{\sharp\}
.

This time we consider the trivial monoid
M=\{1\}
.
Denote

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}^{1}=(q_{i},\{(1,(q_{6},\mathsf{pr}))\},\mathsf{pr})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i\in\{3,4,7\}\,.

We are interested in derivation trees

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,q_{1},(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,q_{2},\sigma_{3}^{1}),\{(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,q_{6},\sigma_{7}^{1})\})\,,

\displaystyle D_{i}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,q_{i},\sigma_{4}^{1})

for
i\in\{3,7\}
, and

\displaystyle D_{4}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{read},q_{4},(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,q_{5},(q_{6},\mathsf{pr})))\,.

The annotated
1
-stack
\mathbf{s}=[(\gamma,\{D_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{3},D_{7}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{1}\})]
is well-formed.
Notice that the run descriptor of
D_{4}
is productive, while run descriptors of
D_{1}
,
D_{3}
, and
D_{7}
are nonproductive.
We can see, though, that
D_{1}
uses both
D_{3}
and
D_{7}
as assumptions, and both
D_{3}
and
D_{7}
use
D_{4}
as an assumption.
In effect
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
counts the nonproductive run descriptor
\mathsf{rd}(D_{4})
twice:

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}([(\gamma,\{D_{4}\}),(\gamma,\{D_{3},D_{7}\})])+\mathsf{low}((\gamma,\{D_{1}\}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}([(\gamma,\{D_{4}\})])+\mathsf{low}((\gamma,\{D_{3}\}))+\mathsf{low}([(\gamma,\{D_{4}\})])+\mathsf{low}((\gamma,\{D_{7}\}))+0

\displaystyle=1+0+1+0+0=2\,.

Remark 19
.

Consider the function
\beth_{n}(k)
defined by

\displaystyle\beth_{0}(k)=k

and

\displaystyle\beth_{n+1}(k)=2^{\beth_{n}(k)}\,.

One can construct an
n
-DPDA
{\mathcal{A}}
that recognizes the language
\{\sharp^{k}a\sharp^{\beth_{n-1}(k)}\colon\,k\in\mathbb{N}\}

(see Blumensath
[
Blu08
, Example 9]
for a very similar construction).
After reading a prefix
\sharp^{k}a
, the number of
0
-stacks in the
n
-stack
s
of
{\mathcal{A}}
is linear in
k
.
It is possible to annotate
s
, resulting in an annotated stack
\mathbf{s}
, such that the maximal annotated run starting from
\mathbf{s}
reads
\beth_{n-1}(k)

\sharp
symbols.
It follows that the
\mathsf{high}
function (and thus
\mathsf{len}
as well) has to be at least
(n-1)
-fold exponential in the size of an annotated
n
-stack.
According to our definition,
\mathsf{high}
and
\mathsf{len}
are (in the worst case)
(n+1)
-fold exponential, which is slightly larger than necessary.
We believe that it is possible to save these two exponentiations, at the cost of complicating proofs.

We now prove Lemma
18
, which fills the rest of this subsection.
We start by proving some (in)equalities regarding the
\mathsf{pow}
function.

Proposition 20
.

The following is true for all positive integers:

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l}))=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},b_{1},\dots,b_{l})\,,

(14)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},\mathsf{pow}(c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{l}),b_{1},\dots,b_{l})\leq

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\leq\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},c_{0},b_{1}c_{1},\dots,b_{l}c_{l})\,,

(15)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},a_{i}^{x},a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k})\leq\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},xa_{k})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i<k\,,

(16)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k})+1\leq\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k}+1)\,,

(17)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\cdot\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})\leq\mathsf{pow}(a_{1}b_{1},\dots,a_{k}b_{k})\,.

(18)

Proof 7.5
.

Equality (
14
) can be shown by induction on
k
.
For
k=0
we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l}))

\displaystyle=(1+\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l}))^{\mathsf{pow}()}-1

\displaystyle=(1+\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l}))^{1}-1=\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l})\,,

and for
k>0
we directly use the induction assumption:

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l}))

\displaystyle=(1+a_{1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{2},\dots,a_{k},\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l}))}-1

\displaystyle=(1+a_{1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{2},\dots,a_{k},b_{1},\dots,b_{l})}-1

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},b_{1},\dots,b_{l})\,.

For Inequality (
15
) suppose first that
k=0
.
By Inequality (
18
), which we prove below, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{l}),b_{1},\dots,b_{l})

\displaystyle=(1+\mathsf{pow}(c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{l}))^{\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l})}-1

\displaystyle=(1+(1+c_{0})^{\mathsf{pow}(c_{1},\dots,c_{l})}-1)^{\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l})}-1

\displaystyle=(1+c_{0})^{\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{l})\cdot\mathsf{pow}(c_{1},\dots,c_{l})}-1

\displaystyle\leq(1+c_{0})^{\mathsf{pow}(b_{1}c_{1},\dots,b_{l}c_{l})}-1=\mathsf{pow}(c_{0},b_{1}c_{1},\dots,b_{l}c_{l})\,.

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{pow}
is monotone, thus the general form of Inequality (
15
) follows from the above special form thanks to Equality (
14
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},\mathsf{pow}(c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{l}),b_{1},\dots,b_{l})

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{pow}(c_{0},c_{1},\dots,c_{l}),b_{1},\dots,b_{l}))

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt\leq\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},\mathsf{pow}(c_{0},b_{1}c_{1},\dots,b_{l}c_{l}))=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},c_{0},b_{1}c_{1},\dots,b_{l}c_{l})\,.

Heading toward proving Inequality (
16
), we first show that

\displaystyle x\cdot\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k})\leq\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k-1},xa_{k})\,,

(19)

where
i<k
, and the numbers
x,a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k}
are positive integers.
This is shown by induction on
k-i
.
When
k-i=1
, we simply have that

\displaystyle x\cdot\mathsf{pow}(a_{k})=x\cdot((1+a_{k})^{1}-1)=(1+xa_{k})^{1}-1=\mathsf{pow}(xa_{k})\,.

Suppose that
k-i>1
.
Notice that
xb\leq b^{x}
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
and
b\geq 2
.
Thus,

\displaystyle x\cdot\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k})

\displaystyle=x\cdot((1+a_{i+1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+2},\dots,a_{k})}-1)

\displaystyle\leq x\cdot(1+a_{i+1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+2},\dots,a_{k})}-1\leq(1+a_{i+1})^{x\cdot\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+2},\dots,a_{k})}-1

\displaystyle\leq(1+a_{i+1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+2},\dots,a_{k-1},xa_{k})}-1=\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k-1},xa_{k})\,.

Above, the first inequality holds because
x\geq 1
;
the second inequality follows from the inequality
xb\leq b^{x}
, where we notice that
(1+a_{i+1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+2},\dots,a_{k})}\geq 2
(because
a_{i+1}\geq 1
and
\mathsf{pow}(\dots)\geq 1
);
the third inequality is an application of the induction assumption.
From Inequality (
19
) it follows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{i}^{x},a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k})

\displaystyle=(1+a_{i}^{x})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k})}-1\leq(1+a_{i})^{x\cdot\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k})}-1

\displaystyle\leq(1+a_{i})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k-1},xa_{k})}-1=\mathsf{pow}(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k-1},xa_{k})\,.

This gives the thesis, by Equality (
14
) and by monotonicity of
\mathsf{pow}
:
