\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},a_{i}^{x},a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k})=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},\mathsf{pow}(a_{i}^{x},a_{i+1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k}))

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt\leq\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},\mathsf{pow}(a_{i},\dots,a_{k-1},xa_{k}))=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},xa_{k})\,.

Inequality (
17
) is shown by induction on
k
.
For
k=1
we simply have

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1})+1=(1+a_{1})^{1}-1+1=(1+a_{1}+1)^{1}-1=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1}+1)\,.

For
k>1
we use the induction assumption as follows (the first inequality below holds because
a_{1}\geq 1
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k})+1

\displaystyle=(1+a_{1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{2},\dots,a_{k})}-1+1\leq(1+a_{1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{2},\dots,a_{k})+1}-1

\displaystyle\leq(1+a_{1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{2},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k}+1)}-1=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1},a_{k}+1)\,.

Inequality (
18
) is also shown by induction on
k
.
For
k=0
the thesis is trivial:

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}()\cdot\mathsf{pow}()=1\cdot 1=1=\mathsf{pow}()\,.

Suppose now that
k\geq 1
, and denote
x=\mathsf{pow}(a_{2},\dots,a_{k})
and
y=\mathsf{pow}(b_{2},\dots,b_{k})
.
We claim that

\displaystyle((1+a_{1})^{x}-1)((1+b_{1})^{y}-1)\leq(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{xy}-1\,.

(20)

Let us prove this inequality.
By symmetry, we can assume that
x\geq y
.
We have three cases.
If
x=y=1
, Inequality (
20
) simply says that

\displaystyle((1+a_{1})^{1}-1)((1+b_{1})^{1}-1)=a_{1}b_{1}\leq(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{1\cdot 1}-1\,.

Next, suppose that
x\geq 2
and
y=1
.
We see that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq(b_{1}-1)^{2}\,,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq b_{1}^{2}-2b_{1}+1\,,

\displaystyle 4b_{1}

\displaystyle\leq b_{1}^{2}+2b_{1}+1\,,

\displaystyle 4b_{1}

\displaystyle\leq(b_{1}+1)^{2}\,,

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{b_{1}+1}{2}\right)^{2}\,.

Because
x\geq 2
and
b_{1}\geq 1
, it follows that

\displaystyle b_{1}\leq\left(\frac{b_{1}+1}{2}\right)^{x}\,.

(21)

Next, observe that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq(a_{1}-1)(b_{1}-1)\,,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq a_{1}b_{1}-a_{1}-b_{1}+1\,,

\displaystyle a_{1}b_{1}+a_{1}+b_{1}+1

\displaystyle\leq 2a_{1}b_{1}+2\,,

\displaystyle(1+a_{1})(b_{1}+1)

\displaystyle\leq 2+2a_{1}b_{1}\,,

\displaystyle(1+a_{1})\left(\frac{b_{1}+1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 1+a_{1}b_{1}\,.

(22)

Using Inequalities (
21
) and (
22
) we obtain Inequality (
20
):

\displaystyle((1+a_{1})^{x}-1)((1+b_{1})^{1}-1)

\displaystyle=(1+a_{1})^{x}\cdot b_{1}-b_{1}\leq(1+a_{1})^{x}\cdot b_{1}-1

\displaystyle\leq(1+a_{1})^{x}\left(\frac{b_{1}+1}{2}\right)^{x}-1\leq(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{x\cdot 1}-1\,.

The remaining case is when
x\geq y\geq 2
.
In this case we have that

\displaystyle((1+a_{1})^{x}-1)((1+b_{1})^{y}-1)

\displaystyle\leq(1+a_{1})^{x}(1+b_{1})^{y}-1\leq(1+a_{1})^{x}(1+b_{1})^{x}-1

\displaystyle\leq(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{x}(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{x}-1=(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{x\cdot 2}-1

\displaystyle\leq(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{x\cdot y}-1\,.

Thus, we have shown Inequality (
20
) in all cases.
Using this inequality and the induction assumption, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\cdot\mathsf{pow}(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})

\displaystyle=((1+a_{1})^{x}-1)((1+b_{1})^{y}-1)\leq(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{x\cdot y}-1

\displaystyle\leq(1+a_{1}b_{1})^{\mathsf{pow}(a_{2}b_{2},\dots,a_{k}b_{k})}-1=\mathsf{pow}(a_{1}b_{1},\dots,a_{k}b_{k})\,.

∎

Heading toward the proof of Lemma
18
, we now observe some auxiliary properties.

Proposition 21
.

Let
\mathbf{s}
be a well-formed annotated stack.
If
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
then
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})=0
and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})=1
; otherwise
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})\geq 1
and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})\geq 2
.

Proof 7.6
.

By induction on the structure of
\mathbf{s}
. In the base cases of a
0
-stack and of an empty
k
-stack, the thesis follows directly from Definition
7.2
.
In the induction step denote
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})\}
(by the definition of types), so

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}\Leftrightarrow\forall_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}\,.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}\Leftrightarrow(\sigma\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}\land\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}})

for
\sigma\in{\mathcal{T}}^{k-1}
(by the definition of
\mathsf{red}^{k}
).
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}\Leftrightarrow\forall_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}(\sigma\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}\land\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}})\,.

If
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
then, by the induction assumption,
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})=0

and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})=1
for all
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
;
in effect

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\big(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}(0+0)=0\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)\,.

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\mathsf{pow}(1,1)=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}1=1\,.

Conversely, if
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\not\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
then, by the induction assumption, at least one among
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})

and
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})
for
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
is positive (and all other are nonnegative);
in effect
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
, being their sum, is positive.
Similarly, at least one among
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})
and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})
is greater than
1
(and all other are positive);
in effect some
\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)
is greater than
1

(notice that
\mathsf{pow}(2,1)=(1+2)^{1}-1=2
, and
\mathsf{pow}(1,2)=(1+1)^{2}-1=3
, and that
\mathsf{pow}
is monotone),
and thus their product
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})
is greater than
1
.
∎

Proposition 22
.

For every well-formed annotated stack
\mathbf{s}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,,\qquad\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,,\qquad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,.

Proof 7.7
.

We analyze Definition
7.2
.
Suppose first that
\mathbf{s}=(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
(i.e., that
\mathbf{s}
is of order
0
).
Because
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})=\{\sigma\}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}|=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}|\{\sigma\}\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}|=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,.

Recall that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{rd}(D)\colon\,D\in\mathfrak{D}\}
, and that
\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}}=(\gamma,\{D\in\mathfrak{D}\colon\,\mathsf{rd}(D)=\sigma\})
; thus,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{D\in\mathfrak{D}\colon\,\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}}C_{0pt(D)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\left(\prod_{D\in\mathfrak{D}\colon\,\mathsf{rd}(D)=\sigma\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}}C_{0pt(D)}\right)=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,,\qquad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{D\in\mathfrak{D}}C_{0pt(D)}=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\left(\prod_{D\in\mathfrak{D}\colon\,\mathsf{rd}(D)=\sigma}C_{0pt(D)}\right)=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,.

If
\mathbf{s}=[\,]
, then
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\emptyset
, and thus

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})=0=\sum_{\sigma\in\emptyset}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,,\qquad\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})=1=\prod_{\sigma\in\emptyset}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,,\qquad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})=1=\prod_{\sigma\in\emptyset}\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,.

Finally, suppose that
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)\colon\,\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})\}
.
Moreover, by well-formedness of
\mathbf{s}
, for every
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})
there is exactly one
\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
such that
\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)=\sigma
;
denote it
\tau_{\sigma}
.
By the definition of a restriction, we have that
\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau_{\sigma}))}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\tau_{\sigma}\}}
.
Recalling that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})=\{\sigma\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\big(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\big(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau_{\sigma}))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\tau_{\sigma}\}})\big)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau))}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau_{\sigma}))}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\tau_{\sigma}\}})\big)=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau))}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})\big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau_{\sigma}))}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\tau_{\sigma}\}})\big)=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\,.

∎

Proposition 23
.

Let
0\leq l\leq k\leq n
, and let
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
be a well-formed annotated
k
-stack that is singular.
In this situation

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=l}^{k}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l}))\,,\qquad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}),\dots,\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{l}))\,.

Proof 7.8
.

Induction on
k-l
.
For
k-l=0
, we simply have
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}
; both sides of each equality are the same (recall that
\mathsf{pow}(x)=x
for every
x
).

Suppose that
k-l\geq 1
, and denote
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\mathbf{s}^{k-2}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
; we have that
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{t}
.
Because
\mathbf{s}
is well-formed and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})
is a singleton, also
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})
is a singleton
\{\sigma\}
, where
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))
.
In effect, restricting
\mathbf{t}
to
\{\sigma\}
or
\mathbf{s}^{k}
to
\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))
does not change the annotated stacks, so, by definition,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})}\big(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t})\,.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}))

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{t}))\,.

From the induction assumption we know that

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=l}^{k-1}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-2}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l}))\,,\qquad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k-2}),\dots,\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{l}))\,.

By substituting this to equalities for
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
,
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})
, and
\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})
we obtain the thesis,
where in the case of
\mathsf{high}
and
\mathsf{len}
we additionally use Equality (
14
).

Proposition 24
.

Let
0\leq l\leq k\leq n
, let
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
be a well-formed annotated
k
-stack, and let
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})
.
In this situation

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\}}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k-1}(\sigma))}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l+1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{l+1}(\sigma))}:\mathbf{s}^{l}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}}\,.

Proof 7.9
.

Proposition
15
used for
\mathbf{s}
implies that

\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\}
and
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})|
;
the latter means that
\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)=\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma^{\prime})
implies
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
for
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})
.

Observe that
\mathsf{top}^{l}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\}})
equals
\mathbf{s}^{l}
restricted to a subset of
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})
.
Proposition
15
used for
\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\}}
implies that this subset is a singleton
\{\sigma^{\prime}\}
,
and that
\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma^{\prime})=\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)
.
This implies that
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
, by the previous paragraph.
Using also Item
(T2)
of Proposition
15
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\}}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k-1}(\sigma))}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l+1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{l+1}(\sigma))}:\mathbf{s}^{l}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}}\,,

as required.

Next, we observe how the functions
\mathsf{low}
,
\mathsf{high}
, and
\mathsf{len}
interplay with composing annotated stacks.

Lemma 25
.

Let
0\leq l\leq k\leq n
, let
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{l};\Psi^{k};f)
be a composer,
and let
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l}
be a well-formed annotated
k
-stack such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})
for each
i\in[l,k]
.
In this situation

\displaystyle\sum_{i=l}^{k}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\leq\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})\,,

(23)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=l}^{k}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})<\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle\mbox{if }f=\mathsf{pr}\,,

(24)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l+1}),\left|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}\right|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\big)\geq\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})\,,

(25)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l+1}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\big)\geq\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle\mbox{if }f=\mathsf{np}\,,

(26)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}),\dots,\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{l+1}),\left|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}\right|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\big)\geq\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})\,.

(27)

Proof 7.10
.

Because
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{l})=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})
, Proposition
15
used for
\mathbf{s}
implies that

\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\colon\,\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})\}
and
|\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})|=|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})|
,
which means that the mapping defined by
\sigma\mapsto\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)
is a bijection between
\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})
and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})
.
Moreover, for
\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\}}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k-1}(\sigma))}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{l+1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{l+1}(\sigma))}:\mathbf{s}^{l}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}}\,,

by Proposition
24
.

For
i\in[l+1,k]
and
\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})
denote
H^{i}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))})
.
Using Proposition
22
, the above property, and Proposition
23
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})=\sum_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{l}(\sigma)\}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\Big(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{l}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}}))+\sum_{i=l+1}^{k}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))})\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{l})+\sum_{i=l+1}^{k}\sum_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))})\,,\qquad\mbox{and}

(28)

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})=\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{pow}\big(H^{k}_{\sigma},H^{k-1}_{\sigma},\dots,H^{l+1}_{\sigma},\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)\,.

(29)

For each
i\in[l+1,k]
it holds that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})=\bigcup\{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))\colon\,\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})\}

(by the definition of a composer, Condition
(C1)
), so, by Proposition
22
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\sum_{\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})\leq\sum_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\sum_{\tau\in\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})=\sum_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))})\,.

(30)

Altogether, Equality (
28
) and Inequality (
30
) used for all
i\in[l+1,k]
imply Inequality (
23
).

For Inequality (
24
), recall from the definition of a composer (Condition
(C4)
) that if
f=\mathsf{pr}
, then for some
i\in[l+1,k]
,
some
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
appears in
\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))
simultaneously for two different
\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})
.
By Proposition
21
,
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})\geq 1
for this
\tau
(because it is productive).
Thus, some positive component
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})

appears in two sums
\sum_{\tau\in\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})
in Inequality (
30
) used for this
i
,
so this inequality (and, in effect, Inequality (
23
)) becomes strict.

Using Equality (
29
), Inequality (
18
), and Proposition
22
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{pow}\big(H^{k}_{\sigma},H^{k-1}_{\sigma},\dots,H^{l+1}_{\sigma},\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{pow}\Big(\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}H^{k}_{\sigma},\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}H^{k-1}_{\sigma},\dots,\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}H^{l+1}_{\sigma},\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}\Big(\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}H^{k}_{\sigma},\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}H^{k-1}_{\sigma},\dots,\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}H^{l+1}_{\sigma},\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\Big)\,.

(31)

Observe that by restricting an annotated stack we can only decrease the value of
\mathsf{high}
.
Thus, for each
i\in[l+1,k]
,

\displaystyle\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}H^{i}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))})

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}))^{|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})|}\leq(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}))^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|}\,.

The last inequality is true because
|\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})|\leq|{\mathcal{T}}^{l}|\leq|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|
.
Using Inequality (
16
) we move the
|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|
exponents (there is at most
n
of them) into the last argument of
\mathsf{pow}

and we obtain Inequality (
25
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{pow}\big((\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}))^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|},(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}))^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|},\dots,(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l+1}))^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|},\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\big)\leq

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l+1}),|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{l})\big)\,.

Now suppose that
f=\mathsf{np}
.
It implies, for each
i\in[l+1,k]
, that each
\tau\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
belongs to the set
\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))
only for one
\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})
,
so all the common factors are equal to
1
(cf. Proposition
21
):

\displaystyle\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}H^{i}_{\sigma}=\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{l})}\prod_{\tau\in\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})=\prod_{\tau\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\tau\}})=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\,.

By substituting this to Inequality (
31
) we obtain Inequality (
26
).

Inequality (
27
) is obtained in the same way as Inequality (
25
), because the definitions of
\mathsf{len}
and
\mathsf{high}
differ only in the base case.
∎

We are now ready to prove Lemma
18
.

Proof 7.11

(Proof of Lemma
18
)
.

It is enough to prove the lemma for annotated runs of length
1
.
Then the result for longer runs follow by an immediate induction.
Thus, assume that
|\mathbf{R}|=1
, and denote
\mathbf{R}(0)=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{0}
with
\mathbf{s}^{0}=(\gamma,\{D\})
.
Recall that our goal is to prove the following inequalities:

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))\leq\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))\geq\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{R}(0))\geq 1+\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,.

We have four cases, depending on the shape of
D
.

Case 1

It is impossible that
D
is of the form
(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,p)
, since then
\mathbf{R}(0)
would not have a successor.

Case 2

Suppose that
D=(\mathsf{read},p,D^{\prime})
.
Then
\mathbf{R}(1)=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}:(\gamma,\{D^{\prime}\})
.
Because both
\mathbf{R}(0)
and
\mathbf{R}(1)
are singular, by Proposition
23
we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(1))=\mathsf{low}((\gamma,\{D^{\prime}\}))+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\,.

Thus, the required inequality about
\mathsf{low}
can be restated as

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})\leq\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{low}((\gamma,\{D^{\prime}\}))\,.

It holds when
\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
(as then
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=0
).
If
\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
, then
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=1
, and either
\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
or the letter read by
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R})
is
\sharp
,
so the right side is positive.

Again by Proposition
23
we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{1}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{0}))\,,\qquad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(1))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{1}),\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\{D^{\prime}\})))\,.

If
\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
, then
\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))=0
and
\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
.
In this case
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\{D^{\prime}\}))=1
, so the two sides of the inequality are equal:

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))=\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,.

If
\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
, then using Inequality (
17
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{1}),C_{0pt(D)})

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{1}),C_{0pt(D^{\prime})}+1)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{1}),C_{0pt(D^{\prime})})+1

\displaystyle\geq\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,.

In the same way we obtain the required inequality for
\mathsf{len}
.

Case 3

Suppose that
D=(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,p,\tau^{k})
.
Then
\mathbf{R}(1)=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k}
.
The operation between
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{R}(0))
and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{R}(1))
is
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
, so
\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))=0
.
For
i\in[1,k-1]
, by Definition
7.1
(
3
) we have that
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D))=\emptyset
,
and by well-formedness of
\mathbf{R}(0)
we have that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D)))
(cf. Proposition
15
);
in effect
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\emptyset
, hence
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=0
and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=1
.
By Definition
7.1
(
3
),
\mathsf{rd}(R)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
, so
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=0
and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=1
; moreover
\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=C_{0}=2
.
Because
\mathbf{R}(0)
and
\mathbf{R}(1)
are singular, by Proposition
23
we can write

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\sum_{i=k}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),1,\dots,1\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k})\big)=\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,,\qquad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),1,\dots,1,2\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),1,\dots,1,1\big)+1

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s}^{k}))+1=1+\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,,

as required.

Case 4

Suppose that
D=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,p,D^{\prime},\mathfrak{D})
.
Let
\alpha
and
k
be such that
\delta(\gamma,p)
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
;
this transition does not read anything, so
\sharp(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))=0
.
By Definition
7.1
(
4
) we have a composer
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{0};\Psi^{k};f)
such that
\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})=\{\mathsf{rd}(E)\colon\,E\in\mathfrak{D}\}
.
Denote also
\Psi^{i}=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime}))
for
i\in[1,k-1]
.
By Definition
7.2
(
4
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}(1)=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{t}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k-1})}:\mathbf{s}^{k-2}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k-2})}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{1})}:(\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\})\,,

where
\mathbf{t}^{k}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k})}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k-1})}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{1})}:(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
.

From Lemma
25
we obtain the following inequality, which is strict if
f=\mathsf{pr}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})})+\mathsf{low}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))\leq\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}^{k})\,.

(32)

Because
\mathbf{R}(0)
is well-formed,
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\mathsf{rd}(D)))
for all
i\in[1,n]
(cf. Proposition
15
).
By Definition
7.1
(
4
),
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)=\Phi^{k}
, and
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)=\Psi^{i}\cup\Phi^{i}
for
i\in[1,k-1]
.
In effect,
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k})
and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\cup\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})
for
i\in[1,k-1]
, so

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k})})\,,

and

(33)

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})+\sum_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i\in[1,k-1]\,,

(34)

where the equalities in the second formula are by Proposition
22
.
Moreover, if
\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\cap\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})\not\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
for some
i\in[1,k-1]
, then the appropriate inequality is strict
(since the component corresponding to
\tau\in\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\cap\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
,
which is positive by Proposition
21
, appears in both sums on the right side,
and only once on the left side).
Because
\mathbf{R}(0)
and
\mathbf{R}(1)
are singular,

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(1))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}^{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})})+\mathsf{low}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))

by Proposition
23
.
We apply (In)equalities (
33
) and (
34
) to the formula for
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))
;
next, we substitute Inequality (
32
); we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})\leq

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}^{k})-\mathsf{low}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(1))-\mathsf{low}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))-\mathsf{low}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})\leq\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(1))+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})\,.

If
\{\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})\}\cup\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})\not\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
, the last inequality is strict, as we have removed negative components.
Because
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})\leq 1
, if some of the above inequalities was strict, we can remove
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})
, and we obtain
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(0))\leq\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{R}(1))
, as required.
On the other hand, if none of these inequalities was strict, then

\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\cap\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
for each
i\in[1,k-1]
, and
f=\mathsf{np}
, and
\{\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})\}\cup\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
;
from Definition
7.1
(
4
) it follows that in this case
\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
, so
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=0
, and we obtain the required inequality as well.

Next, we prove the inequality for
\mathsf{high}
.
Denote

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i\in[k+1,n]\,,

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i\in[1,k-1]\,,

\displaystyle b_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})})

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i\in[1,k]\,.

Suppose first that
\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
.
Then, by Definition
7.1
(
4
),
\{\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})\}\cup\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
, and
f=\mathsf{np}
,
and
\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\cap\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
for each
i\in[1,k-1]
. In consequence,
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))=\mathsf{high}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))=1
.
Recall that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\cup\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})
for
i\in[1,k-1]
.
Thus, by Proposition
22
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})=\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\cdot\prod_{\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})}\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\hskip-100.00015pt

\displaystyle=a_{i}\cdot b_{i}

\displaystyle\mbox{for }i\in[1,k-1]\,;

(35)

the second equality above holds because for
\sigma\in\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})\cap\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
we have
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})=1

(cf. Proposition
21
).
Because
f=\mathsf{np}
, from Lemma
25
we know that

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}(b_{k},b_{k-1},\dots,b_{1},1)=\mathsf{pow}\big(b_{k},b_{k-1},\dots,b_{1},\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))\big)\geq\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}^{k})\,.

Using Proposition
23
and Equalities (
35
), then Inequality (
15
),
then the above inequality, and then again Proposition
23
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},b_{k},a_{k-1}b_{k-1},a_{k-2}b_{k-2},\dots,a_{1}b_{1},1)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},\mathsf{pow}(b_{k},b_{k-1},\dots,b_{1},1),a_{k-1},a_{k-2}\dots,a_{1},1)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}^{k}),a_{k-1},a_{k-2},\dots,a_{1},1)=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,.

Next, suppose that
\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
.
Then Lemma
25
gives us the inequality

\displaystyle\mathsf{pow}\big(b_{k},b_{k-1},\dots,b_{1},|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))\big)\geq\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}^{k})\,.

(36)

By definition it holds

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{0})

\displaystyle=C_{0pt(D)}=(2|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|)^{n}\cdot(C_{0pt(D)-1})^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|+1}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{k-1}\cdot|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|^{n}\cdot C_{0pt(D^{\prime})}\cdot\prod_{E\in\mathfrak{D}}C_{0pt(E)}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{k-1}\cdot|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{high}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))\cdot\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D})).

Using Inequality (
16
) we replace
2^{k-1}
in the last argument of
\mathsf{pow}
by
2
in the
k-1
previous arguments;
then we observe that
(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{i}))^{2}\geq a_{i}b_{i}
for each
i\in[1,k-1]
;
then we use Inequality (
15
), and finally Inequality (
36
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(0))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{1}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{0})\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{1}),

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt2^{k-1}\cdot|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{high}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))\cdot\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}),(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}))^{2},(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-2}))^{2},\dots,

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{1}))^{2},|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{high}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))\cdot\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}\big(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},b_{k},a_{k-1}b_{k-1},a_{k-2}b_{k-2},\dots,a_{1}b_{1},

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{high}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))\cdot\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}\big(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},\mathsf{pow}\!\left(b_{k},b_{k-1},\dots,b_{1},|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|^{n}\cdot\mathsf{high}((\gamma,\mathfrak{D}))\right),

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pta_{k-1},a_{k-2},\dots,a_{1},\mathsf{high}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}\big(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}^{k}),a_{k-1},a_{k-2},\dots,a_{1},\mathsf{high}((\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\}))\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{R}(1))\,.

The inequality for
\mathsf{len}
can be proved in a very similar way as that for
\mathsf{high}
in the case
\mathsf{rd}(D)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pr}}
.
∎

7.3.
Relating Upper and Lower Bounds

As already mentioned, it is meaningful to consider the functions
\mathsf{low}
and
\mathsf{high}
because they are closely related: one is bounded if the other is bounded.
This is shown in the following proposition.

Proposition 26
.

There exists a function
H\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for each configuration
c
and each run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(c)
there exists
a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}
for which
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))=\{\sigma\}
, and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})=c
, and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})\leq H(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}))
.

Proof 7.12
.

Let
d
be a number such that for each derivation tree there exists a derivation tree with the same conclusion and depth at most
d
;
such a number exists, because there are only finitely many possible conclusions.
For each
k\geq 0
we define a function
N_{k}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, and we take
H=N_{n}
.
The definition is inductive:
N_{k}(0)=1
, and, for
L>0
,

\displaystyle N_{0}(L)=(C_{d})^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|}\,,

\displaystyle N_{k}(L)=\big(\mathsf{pow}(N_{k}(L-1),N_{k-1}(L))\big)^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{k-1}|}

\displaystyle\mbox{for }k>0\,,

where
C_{d}
is the constant from Definition
7.2
.

By definition of a type, for each configuration
c
and each run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(c)
there exists a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}
such that

\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))=\{\sigma\}
and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})=c
.
We can assume without loss of generality that all derivation trees in
\mathbf{s}
have depth at most
d
:
we can safely replace each tree by another (smaller) tree having the same conclusion.
Thus, it is enough to prove that for each well-formed annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{s}
, in which all derivation trees have depth at most
d
,
it holds
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})\leq N_{k}(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}))
.

Denote
L=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
.
If
L=0
then
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})=1=N_{k}(L)
, thanks to Proposition
21
.
Suppose that
L>0
.
In this case we prove the thesis by induction on the structure of
\mathbf{s}
.
For a stack
\mathbf{s}=(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
of order
0
it holds

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})\leq\prod_{D\in\mathfrak{D}}C_{0pt(D)}\leq(C_{d})^{|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|}=N_{0}(L)\,.

Next, consider a stack
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})
equals the sum of
\mathsf{low}
for
\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))}
and
\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}}

over all
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
.
We have two cases.
One possibility is that
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})=L
for some
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
.
Then
\mathsf{low}
for all other considered stacks is
0
, so their
\mathsf{high}
is
1
.
Using the induction assumption we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})\leq\mathsf{pow}(N_{k}(L),1)\cdot\prod_{\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})\setminus\{\sigma\}}\mathsf{pow}(1,1)=N_{k}(L)\,.

The opposite situation is that
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})\leq L-1
for each
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
.
Observing that
N_{k}
is monotone, by the induction assumption

\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))})\leq N_{k}(L-1)
and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\{\sigma\}})\leq N_{k-1}(L)
for each
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})
,
so we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s})\leq\prod_{\sigma\in\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k-1})}\mathsf{pow}(N_{k}(L-1),N_{k-1}(L))\leq N_{k}(L)\,.

∎

7.4.
Assumptions Are Used in Returns

Our next goal is to formally prove that whenever an assumption of a run descriptor is used in an annotated run, then we have a return.

Lemma 27
.

Let
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{0}
be a well-formed singular annotated
n
-stack, where
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=\{\sigma\}
.
If
(m,\xi)\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)
, then there exists an annotated run
\mathbf{R}
starting in
\mathbf{s}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R})
is an
r
-return,
\varphi(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{R}))=m
,
and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathbf{R}(|\mathbf{R}|))=\mathbf{s}^{r}{\restriction}_{\{\xi\}}
.

Proof 7.13
.

We use induction on
\mathsf{len}(\mathbf{s})
.
Thanks to Lemma
18
we can always use the induction assumption for the successor of
\mathbf{s}
.
We have several cases depending on the shape of the derivation tree
D
in
\mathbf{s}^{0}
(that is, on the first operation in an annotated run starting in
\mathbf{s}
).
We use the characterization of returns from Proposition
10
.

Case 1

If
D=(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,p)
then
\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)=\emptyset
, so the assumptions cannot hold.

Case 2

Suppose that
D=(\mathsf{read},p,D^{\prime})
.
Then the successor
\mathbf{t}
of
\mathbf{s}
differs from
\mathbf{s}
only in the topmost
0
-stack;
the new topmost
0
-stack has type
\{\tau\}
such that
\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)=\varphi(a)\circ\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)
,
where
a
is the letter read by the step between
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
.
Because
(m,\xi)\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)
, there exists
m^{\prime}
such that
m=\varphi(a)\cdot m^{\prime}
and
(m^{\prime},\xi)\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)
.
By the induction assumption for
\mathbf{t}
,
there exists an annotated run
\mathbf{S}
starting in
\mathbf{t}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
is an
r
-return,
\varphi(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S}))=m^{\prime}
,
and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathbf{S}(|\mathbf{S}|))=\mathbf{s}^{r}{\restriction}_{\{\xi\}}
.
Together with the step between
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{t}
, it gives us an annotated run as required.

Case 3

Suppose that
D=(\mathsf{pop},\gamma,p,\tau)
, where
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{k}
.
The successor of
\mathbf{s}
is
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)=\emptyset
for
i<k
, so
r\geq k
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)=\{(\mathbf{1}_{M},\tau)\}
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma))=\{\tau\}
by well-formedness of
\mathbf{s}
(cf. Proposition
15
).
If
r=k
, then
(m,\xi)=(\mathbf{1}_{M},\tau)
.
In this case the annotated run of length
1
satisfies the thesis.
Otherwise
r>k
, and
(m,\xi)\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)=\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)
.
Then as well
(m,\xi)\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau^{\prime})
, where
\tau^{\prime}
is the run descriptor in the type of
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{k})
(since
\tau=\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau^{\prime})
).
The induction assumption for
\mathbf{t}
gives us an annotated run
\mathbf{S}
starting in
\mathbf{t}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
is an
r
-return,
\varphi(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S}))=m
,
and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathbf{S}(|\mathbf{S}|))=\mathbf{s}^{r}{\restriction}_{\{\xi\}}
.
Together with the step between
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{t}
, it gives us an annotated run as required.

Case 4

Suppose that
D=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,p,D^{\prime},\mathfrak{D})
.
Let
\alpha
,
k
,
\Psi^{i}
,
\Phi^{i}
be as in Definition
7.1
(
4
).
The successor of
\mathbf{s}
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{t}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k-1})}:\mathbf{s}^{k-2}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k-2})}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Psi^{1})}:(\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\})\,,

where
\mathbf{t}^{k}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k})}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{k-1})}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\Phi^{1})}:(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
.
Recall from our previous proofs that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})
for
i\in[k+1,n]
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}^{k})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})
.
If
(m,\xi)\in\Psi^{r}
and
r\neq k
, then the induction assumption for
\mathbf{t}
gives us an annotated run
\mathbf{S}
starting in
\mathbf{t}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
is an
r
-return,
\varphi(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S}))=m
,
and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathbf{S}(|\mathbf{S}|))=\mathbf{s}^{r}{\restriction}_{\{\xi\}}
; together with the step between
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{t}
, it gives us an annotated run as required.
Otherwise
(m,\xi)\in\Phi^{r}
and
r\leq k
.
Recall that we have a composer
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{0};\Psi^{k};f)
.
The definition of a composer (Condition
(C1)
) gives us some
(m_{1},\tau)\in\Phi^{0}
and
m_{2}\in M
such that
m=m_{1}\cdot m_{2}
and
(m_{2},\xi)\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)
.
We see that
(m_{1},\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\in\Psi^{k}
(by Condition
(C2)
of the definition).
The induction assumption for
\mathbf{t}
and
(m_{1},\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\in\Psi^{k}
gives us an annotated run
\mathbf{S}
starting in
\mathbf{t}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
is a
k
-return,
\varphi(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S}))=m_{1}
,
and
\mathsf{top}^{k}(\mathbf{S}(|\mathbf{S}|))=\mathbf{t}^{k}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)\}}
.
By Proposition
24
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{k}{\restriction}_{\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)\}}=\mathbf{s}^{k}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\tau))}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k-1}(\tau))}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}{\restriction}_{\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{1}(\tau))}:(\gamma,\mathfrak{D}){\restriction}_{\{\tau\}}\,.

Recalling that
r\leq k
,
the induction assumption for
\mathbf{S}(|\mathbf{S}|)
and
(m_{2},\xi)\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)
gives us an annotated run
\mathbf{T}
starting in
\mathbf{S}(|\mathbf{S}|)
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{T})
is an
r
-return,
\varphi(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{T}))=m_{2}
,
and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathbf{T}(|\mathbf{T}|))=\mathbf{s}^{r}{\restriction}_{\{\xi\}}
.
The step between
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{t}
composed with
\mathbf{S}
and then with
\mathbf{T}
gives us an annotated run as required.
∎

7.5.
Completeness of Types

In the previous subsection we have proved soundness of the type system, which means that if a run descriptor is contained in the type of a configuration
then a corresponding run exists from this configuration.
As usual, we need the opposite direction (completeness) as well;
that is, having a run from a configuration, we want to imply that the corresponding run descriptor is in the type of this configuration.
This is shown in Lemma
28
, which is a converse of Lemma
27
.
While reversing Lemma
27
we have to remember that not every run can be extended to an annotated run,
so in Lemma
28
we need to use runs, not annotated runs.

Lemma 28
.

Let
R
be an
r
-return, and let
\xi\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(|R|))
.
Then there exists a run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
such that
(\varphi(R),\mathsf{red}^{r}(\xi))\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)
.

Before proving Lemma
28
, we first state four auxiliary lemmas.
These lemmas are used not only in the proof of Lemma
28
, but also in the next subsection.
Actually, every of these lemmas has a part denoted by
(\star)
; these parts are needed only in the next subsection.

Lemma 29
.

Let
R
be a run of length
1
whose transition is
\mathsf{read}
, and let
\tau\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(1))
.
Then there exists
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
such that
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)=\varphi(R)\circ\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
for each
i\in[1,n]
.
Moreover, there exists a well-formed singular annotated
0
-stack
\mathbf{v}^{0}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{v}^{0})=\{\sigma\}
, and the following is satisfied.

(\star)

Let
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
be a well-formed annotated
n
-stack with
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
Then there exists an annotated run
\mathbf{S}
of length
1
such that
\mathbf{S}(0)=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, the transition of
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
is
\mathsf{read}
,
it reads the same letter as the transition of
R
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1)))=\{\tau\}
.

Proof 7.14
.

By definition of
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}
,
there exists a well-formed annotated stack
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}:(\gamma,\{D^{\prime}\})
such that
\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})=\tau
and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})=R(1)
.
Well-formedness of
\mathbf{s}
implies that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau))
for each
i\in[1,n]
(cf. Proposition
15
).
When
p
is the state of
R(0)
, Definition
7.1
(
2
) implies that
D=(\mathsf{read},p,D^{\prime})
is a derivation tree with conclusion
\gamma\vdash\sigma
, where

\sigma=(p,\Phi^{n},\Phi^{n-1},\dots,\Phi^{1},f)
and
\Phi^{i}=\varphi(R)\circ\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
for each
i\in[1,n]
.
Because
\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma))=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau))
,
the annotated stack
\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}:(\gamma,\{D\})
is well-formed (again, cf. Proposition
15
),
so
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
.

In order to prove Property
(\star)
, as
\mathbf{v}^{0}
we take
(\gamma,\{D\})
.
Clearly
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{v}^{0})=\{\mathsf{rd}(D)\}=\{\sigma\}
.
Consider now any well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{S}
be the annotated run from
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
to its successor.
Because the topmost
0
-stack of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is annotated by
D=(\mathsf{read},p,D^{\prime})
, the successor of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
indeed exists,
and the transition of
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
is
\mathsf{read}
.
The state of
R(1)
and the state of
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{S}(1))
are the same (namely
\pi_{1}(\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime}))
), so
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
reads the same letter as
R
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1))=(\gamma,\{D^{\prime}\})
, so
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1)))=\{\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})\}=\{\tau\}
, as required.
∎

Lemma 30
.

Let
R
be a run of length
1
performing
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
, and let
\tau\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(1))
.
Then there exists
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
such that
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
for each
i\in[k+1,n]
,
and
\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\sigma)=\{(\mathbf{1}_{M},\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\}
.
Moreover, there exists a well-formed singular annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{v}^{k}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{v}^{k}))=\{\sigma\}
, and the following is satisfied.

(\star)

Let
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
be a well-formed annotated
n
-stack with
\mathsf{top}^{k}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}^{k}
.
Then there exists an annotated run
\mathbf{S}
of length
1
such that
\mathbf{S}(0)=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
performs
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1)))=\{\tau\}
.

Proof 7.15
.

Denote
R(0)=(p,s^{n}:s^{n-1}:\dots:s^{0})
; then
\pi_{2}(R(1))=s^{n}:s^{n-1}:\dots:s^{k}
.
By definition of
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}
, there exists a well-formed annotated stack
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k}

such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{k}))=\{\tau\}
,
and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{i})
for each
i\in[k,n]
.
Then, by Proposition
15
,
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)\}
.
Well-formedness of
\mathbf{s}
implies that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau))
for each
i\in[k+1,n]
(cf. Proposition
15
).
For
i\in[1,k-1]
let
\mathbf{s}^{i}
be the well-formed annotated
i
-stack such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{i})
(we annotate
s^{i}
by empty sets).
Finally, by Definition
7.1
(
3
),
D=(\mathsf{pop},s^{0},p,\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))
is a derivation tree with conclusion
\gamma\vdash\sigma
,
where
\gamma=\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{0})
and

\displaystyle\sigma=(p,\mathsf{ass}^{n}(\tau),\mathsf{ass}^{n-1}(\tau),\dots,\mathsf{ass}^{k+1}(\tau),\{(\mathbf{1}_{M},\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)\},\emptyset,\dots,\emptyset,\mathsf{np})\,,

so
\mathbf{s}^{0}=(\gamma,\{D\})
has type
\{\sigma\}
.
Using Proposition
15
we observe that
\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{0}
is well-formed, so
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
.

In order to prove Property
(\star)
, as
\mathbf{v}^{k}
we take
\mathbf{s}^{k}:\mathbf{s}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{0}
.
Clearly
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{v}^{k}))=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{0})=\{\sigma\}
.
Consider now any well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{top}^{k}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}^{k}
.
Let
\mathbf{S}
be the annotated run from
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
to its successor.
Because the topmost
0
-stack of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is annotated by
D
, the successor of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
indeed exists, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
performs
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{top}^{k}(\mathbf{S}(1))=\mathbf{s}^{k}
, so
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1)))=\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{k}))=\{\tau\}
.
∎

Lemma 31
.

Let
R
be a run of length
1
performing
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
, and let
\tau\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(1))
.
Then there exists
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
such that
\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
for each
i\in[1,n]\setminus\{k\}
.
Moreover, there exists a well-formed singular annotated
0
-stack
\mathbf{v}^{0}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{v}^{0})=\{\sigma\}
, and the following is satisfied.

(\star)

Let
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
be a well-formed annotated
n
-stack with
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
Then there exists an annotated run
\mathbf{S}
of length
1
such that
\mathbf{S}(0)=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1)))=\{\tau\}
.

Proof 7.16
.

Before starting the actual proof, we observe that for each pair of well-formed annotated stacks
\mathbf{s}
,
\mathbf{t}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s})=\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{t})

we can construct a well-formed annotated stack
\mathbf{s}\oplus\mathbf{t}
whose type is
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})\cup\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})
and such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}\oplus\mathbf{t})=\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s})
.
We construct
\mathbf{s}\oplus\mathbf{t}
by induction on the structure of
\mathbf{s}
.
Denote
\widetilde{\Psi}=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t})\setminus\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s})
.
If
\mathbf{s}
is of order
0
, then we take
\mathbf{s}\oplus\mathbf{t}=(\gamma,\mathfrak{D}\cup\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
, where
\mathbf{s}=(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
and
\mathbf{t}{\restriction}_{\widetilde{\Psi}}=(\gamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
.
If
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{t}=[\,]
, then
\mathbf{s}\oplus\mathbf{t}=[\,]
is fine.
If
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{j}:\mathbf{s}^{j-1}
and
\mathbf{t}{\restriction}_{\widetilde{\Psi}}=\mathbf{t}^{j}:\mathbf{t}^{j-1}
, then as
\mathbf{s}\oplus\mathbf{t}
we take
(\mathbf{s}^{j}\oplus\mathbf{t}^{j}):(\mathbf{s}^{j-1}\oplus\mathbf{t}^{j-1})
;
observe that it is well-formed, because the types of
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{t}{\restriction}_{\widetilde{\Psi}}
are disjoint.

Denote
R(0)=(p,s^{n}:s^{n-1}:\dots:s^{1}:(\gamma,x))
; then
\pi_{2}(R(1))
equals

\displaystyle s^{n}:s^{n-1}:\dots:s^{k+1}:(s^{k}:s^{k-1}:\dots:s^{1}:(\gamma,x)):\mathsf{p}_{+1}(s^{k-1}:s^{k-2}:\dots:s^{1}:(\alpha,x))\,.

By definition of
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}
, there exists a well-formed annotated stack
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}:(\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\})

in which
\mathbf{s}^{k}=\mathbf{t}^{k}:\mathbf{t}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{t}^{1}:(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
,
such that
\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})=\tau
, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{i})
for each
i\in[1,n]\setminus\{k\}
, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{t}^{i})=\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{i})
for each
i\in[1,k]
.
Denote
\Psi^{i}=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)
for each
i\in[1,n]
.
Well-formedness of
\mathbf{s}
implies that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})
for each
i\in[1,n]
(cf. Proposition
15
),
and, thanks to Proposition
16
, there exists a composer
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{0};\Psi^{k};f)
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})
for each
i\in[1,k]

and
\{\mathsf{rd}(E)\colon\,E\in\mathfrak{D}\}=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
.
By Definition
7.1
(
4
),
D=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,p,D^{\prime},\mathfrak{D})
is a derivation tree with conclusion
\gamma\vdash\sigma
, where

\displaystyle\sigma=(p,\Psi^{n},\Psi^{n-1},\dots,\Psi^{k+1},\Phi^{k},\Psi^{k-1}\cup\Phi^{k-1},\Psi^{k-2}\cup\Phi^{k-2},\dots,\Psi^{1}\cup\Phi^{1},g)\,.

Using Proposition
15
we observe that the annotated stack

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{t}^{k}:(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}\oplus\mathbf{t}^{k-1}):(\mathbf{s}^{k-2}\oplus\mathbf{t}^{k-2}):\dots:(\mathbf{s}^{1}\oplus\mathbf{t}^{1}):(\gamma,\{D\})

is well-formed, so
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
.

In order to prove Property
(\star)
, as
\mathbf{v}^{0}
we take
(\gamma,\{D\})
.
Clearly
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{v}^{0})=\{\mathsf{rd}(D)\}=\{\sigma\}
.
Consider now any well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{S}
be the annotated run from
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
to its successor.
Because the topmost
0
-stack of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is annotated by
D
, the successor of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
indeed exists, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1))=(\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\})
, so
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1)))=\{\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})\}=\{\tau\}
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

Let
R
be a run in which
R{\restriction}_{0,1}
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
and
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
is a
k
-return, let
\tau\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(|R|))
,
and let
\rho\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(1))
be such that
(\varphi(R),\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\in\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\rho)
.
Then there exists
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
such that
\varphi(R)\circ\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\sigma)
for each
i\in[1,k]
.
Moreover, there exists a well-formed singular annotated
0
-stack
\mathbf{v}^{0}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{v}^{0})=\{\sigma\}
, and the following is satisfied.

(\star)

Let
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
be a well-formed annotated
n
-stack with
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
Then there exists an annotated run
\mathbf{S}
of length
1
such that
\mathbf{S}(0)=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1)))=\{\rho\}
,
and
\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathsf{pop}^{k}(\mathbf{S}(1))))
.

Proof 7.17
.

Denote
R(0)=(p,s^{n}:s^{n-1}:\dots:s^{1}:(\gamma,x))
; then
\pi_{2}(R(1))
is as in the previous lemma,
and
\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(0))\cong\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(|R|))
(due to Proposition
8
).
The definition of
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}
gives us a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{u}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{u}))=\{\tau\}

and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{u})=R(|R|)
,
and a well-formed annotated stack
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{1}:(\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\})

such that
\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})=\rho
and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})=R(1)
.
Denote
\mathbf{u}^{k}=\mathsf{top}^{k}(\mathbf{u})
.
Then
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{u}^{k}))=\{\tau\}
, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{u}^{k})=\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{k}:s^{k-1}:\dots:s^{1}:(\gamma,x))
, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{i})
for each
i\in[1,n]\setminus\{k\}
,
and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{k}:s^{k-1}:\dots:s^{1}:(\gamma,x))
.
Denote
\Psi^{i}=\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\rho)
for each
i\in[1,n]
.
Well-formedness of
\mathbf{s}
implies that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{s}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Psi^{i})
for each
i\in[1,n]
(cf. Proposition
15
).
By Proposition
15
,
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{u}^{k})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)\}
.
Thanks to the assumption
\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)\in\pi_{2}(\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\rho))
we have that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{u}^{k})\subseteq\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})
.
In effect, the annotated stack
\mathbf{u}^{k}\oplus\mathbf{s}^{k}
has type
\pi_{2}(\Psi^{k})
,
equal to the type of
\mathbf{s}^{k}
, but additionally
\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{u}^{k}\oplus\mathbf{s}^{k}))

(recalling the construction from the previous proof,
we see that to
\mathbf{u}^{k}\oplus\mathbf{s}^{k}
we take all annotations from
\mathbf{u}^{k}
and some annotations from
\mathbf{s}^{k}
).
Denote
\mathbf{u}^{k}\oplus\mathbf{s}^{k}=\mathbf{t}^{k}:\mathbf{t}^{k-1}:\dots:\mathbf{t}^{1}:(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
.
By Proposition
16
we have a composer
(\Phi^{k},\Phi^{k-1},\dots,\Phi^{0};\Psi^{k};f)
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{t}^{i})=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{i})
for each
i\in[1,k]

and
\{\mathsf{rd}(E)\colon\,E\in\mathfrak{D}\}=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
.
Because
\tau\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
and
(\varphi(R),\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\in\Psi^{k}
, it holds
(\varphi(R),\tau)\in\Phi^{0}

(thanks to Conditions
(C2)
and
(C3)
of the definition of a composer),
which implies
\varphi(R)\circ\mathsf{ass}^{i}(\tau)\subseteq\Phi^{i}
for each
i\in[1,k]
(thanks to Condition
(C1)
of the definition).
By Definition
7.1
(
4
),
D=(\mathsf{push},\gamma,p,D^{\prime},\mathfrak{D})
is a derivation tree with conclusion
\gamma\vdash\sigma
, where

\displaystyle\sigma=(p,\Psi^{n},\Psi^{n-1},\dots,\Psi^{k+1},\Phi^{k},\Psi^{k-1}\cup\Phi^{k-1},\Psi^{k-2}\cup\Phi^{k-2},\dots,\Psi^{1}\cup\Phi^{1},g)\,.

We observe that the annotated stack

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{t}^{k}:(\mathbf{s}^{k-1}\oplus\mathbf{t}^{k-1}):(\mathbf{s}^{k-2}\oplus\mathbf{t}^{k-2}):\dots:(\mathbf{s}^{1}\oplus\mathbf{t}^{1}):(\gamma,\{D\})

is well-formed (by Proposition
15
), so
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
.

In order to prove Property
(\star)
, as
\mathbf{v}^{0}
we take
(\gamma,\{D\})
.
Clearly
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{v}^{0})=\{\mathsf{rd}(D)\}=\{\sigma\}
.
Consider now any well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{S}
be the annotated run from
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
to its successor.
Because the topmost
0
-stack of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is annotated by
D
, the successor of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
indeed exists, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1))=(\alpha,\{D^{\prime}\})
, so
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{S}(1)))=\{\mathsf{rd}(D^{\prime})\}=\{\rho\}
.
On the other hand,
\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathsf{pop}^{k}(\mathbf{S}(1)))
is
(\gamma,\mathfrak{D})
,
so its type is
\{\mathsf{rd}(E)\colon\,E\in\mathfrak{D}\}=\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
, and we know that
\tau\in\pi_{2}(\Phi^{0})
.
∎

Proof 7.18

(Proof of Lemma
28
)
.

Recall that we are given an
r
-return
R
, and a run descriptor
\xi\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(|R|))
,
and we have to show existence of a run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
such that
(\varphi(R),\mathsf{red}^{r}(\xi))\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)
.
We use induction on the length of the
r
-return
R
.
Proposition
10
gives us possible forms of
R
; we analyze these cases.

Suppose first that
|R|=1
and the only transition of
R
performs
\mathsf{pop}^{r}
.
We take
\sigma
from Lemma
30
, where we take
\xi
as
\tau
and
r
as
k
.
By assumption
\varphi(R)=\mathbf{1}_{M}
, so
(\varphi(R),\mathsf{red}^{r}(\xi))\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)
.

Next, suppose that
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
is an
r
-return, and the first transition of
R
is
\mathsf{read}
, or performs
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
for
k<r
, or
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
for
k\neq r
.
The induction assumption for
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
gives us a run descriptor
\tau\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(1))
such that
(\varphi(R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}),\mathsf{red}^{r}(\xi))\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)
,
and Lemma
29
, or
30
, or
31
, respectively,
used for
R{\restriction}_{0,1}
gives us a run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
such that
\varphi(R{\restriction}_{0,1})\circ\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)

(where
\varphi(R{\restriction}_{0,1})
may be nontrivial only when the transition is
\mathsf{read}
).

Finally, suppose that the first transition of
R
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
for
k\geq r
and
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}=S\circ T
for some
k
-return
S
and
r
-return
T
.
The induction assumption for
T
gives us a run descriptor
\tau\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(T(0))
such that
(\varphi(T),\mathsf{red}^{r}(\xi))\in\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)
, and
the induction assumption for
S
gives us a run descriptor
\rho\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(1))
such that
(\varphi(S),\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\in\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\rho)
.
Using Lemma
32
for
R{\restriction}_{0,1}\circ S
we obtain a run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
such that
\varphi(S)\circ\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)

(recalling that
r\leq k
), so
(\varphi(R),\mathsf{red}^{r}(\xi))=(\varphi(S)\circ\varphi(T),\mathsf{red}^{r}(\xi))\in\varphi(S)\circ\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\tau)\subseteq\mathsf{ass}^{r}(\sigma)
.
∎

7.6.
Reproducing Upper Runs

Till now we were using types to describe returns from a configuration, but thanks to the decomposition given by Proposition
9
we can also describe
r
-upper runs.
This is stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 33
.

Let
R
be an
r
-upper run (where
r\in[0,n]
), and let
\tau\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(|R|))
.
Then there exists a run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))

and a monotone function
f_{R}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that the following is satisfied.

(\star)

Let
\mathbf{s}
be a well-formed annotated
n
-stack such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))=\{\sigma\}

and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}))\allowbreak\cong\mathsf{top}^{r}(R(0))
.
Then there exists a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{t}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{t}))\allowbreak=\{\tau\}
,
and there exists a run
S
from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
that is
(r,\varphi)
-parallel to
R
, and such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})\leq f_{R}(\sharp(S)+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}))\,,

and

\displaystyle f_{R}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}))\geq\sharp(S)+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t})\,.

The idea staying behind a proof of this lemma is that the run
R
can be split into parts of two kinds.
First, we have parts for which the topmost
r
-stack of
R(0)
is responsible.
Since in
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
the topmost
r
-stack is the same, we can execute them also from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
.
Second, we have parts not controlled by the topmost
r
-stack of
R(0)
, but (according to Proposition
9
) these are returns.
Analogous returns can be executed from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
, because of the run descriptor
\sigma
in
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))
.

Two aspects of the statement of the lemma can be understood basing on the above idea.
First, the correction function
f_{R}
is really needed (i.e., the lemma would be false if the identity function was always taken as
f_{R}
).
Second, there does not need to exist an annotated run from
\mathbf{s}
to
\mathbf{t}
; we only prove existence of a (non-annotated) run from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
.
The justification of both these phenomena is the same:
while creating the run from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
, we completely ignore the annotations contained in the topmost
r
-stack of
\mathbf{s}

(while an annotated run from
\mathbf{s}
necessarily follows them);
instead, as long as the topmost
r
-stack of
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s})
controls the run, we copy steps of the run
R

(only after leaving the topmost
r
-stack, we start using the annotations from
\mathbf{s}
).
In a sense,
f_{R}
describes how much can be lost while ignoring annotations in the topmost
r
-stack of
\mathbf{s}

(recall that, while ignoring annotation, this
r
-stack is the same as in
R(0)
, thus fixed; only the annotations are not fixed).

Before proving Lemma
33
we show how Theorem
11
follows from it.
For this purpose the inequalities regarding
\mathsf{low}
and
\mathsf{high}
are redundant; they are used later to prove Theorem
12
.

Proof 7.19

(Proof of Theorem
11
)
.

Recall that we are given a
k
-upper run
R
, and a configuration
c
having the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same positionless topmost
k
-stack as
R(0)
.
Consider the run descriptor
\tau=(\pi_{1}(R(|R|)),\emptyset,\dots,\emptyset,\mathsf{np})
and observe that
\tau\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(|R|))

(we annotate the topmost
0
-stack of
R(|R|)
by the derivation tree from Definition
7.1
(
1
)).
Applying Lemma
33
we obtain a run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))=\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(c)
.
Then we take any well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))=\{\sigma\}
and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})=c
,
existing by the definition of
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}
.
Since
\mathsf{top}^{k}(\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}))\cong\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(0))
,
from Property
(\star)
of Lemma
33
we obtain a run
S
that starts in
c
and is
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
R
, as required.
∎

Below, we give an auxiliary lemma, showing how to construct a function
f_{R}
needed for Lemma
33
.

Lemma 34
.

Let
k\in[0,n]
, and let
\mathbf{v}^{k}
be a well-formed singular annotated
k
-stack.
Then there exists a monotone function
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all well-formed singular annotated
n
-stacks
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
with

\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{s}^{k}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{v}^{k}
and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{v}^{k})

it holds that
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})\leq f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime}))
and
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}))\geq\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})
.

Proof 7.20
.

We first define the function
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}
, and then we show that it satisfies the thesis.
Suppose that
k
and
\mathbf{v}^{k}
are fixed.
We construct
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(N)
by induction on
N
.
Consider some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
First, we ensure that
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(N)\geq N+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})
for all annotated
k
-stacks
\mathbf{s}^{k}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{v}^{k})
.
This is possible, because
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})
equals the number of productive run descriptors altogether in the types of all
0
-stacks in
\mathbf{s}^{k}
.
So, although there are infinitely many annotated
k
-stacks
\mathbf{s}^{k}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{v}^{k})
,
the value of
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})
is bounded by the number of
0
-stacks in
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{k})
times
|{\mathcal{T}}^{0}|
.
Next, we ensure that
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(N)\geq\mathsf{pow}(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{v}^{k}))
for all tuples
(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},a_{k})
of positive integers
such that
\mathsf{pow}(a_{n},a_{n-1},\dots,a_{k+1},a_{k})=N
.
Notice that there are only finitely many such tuples (in particular, none of
a_{i}
may be greater than
N
).
Finally, we ensure that
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(N)\geq f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(N-1)
(unless
N=0
), in order to ensure monotonicity of
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}

(since we are defining
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}
by induction,
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(N-1)
is already defined).

Consider now well-formed singular annotated
n
-stacks
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{s}^{k}
,
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{v}^{k}
, and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{s}^{k})=\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{v}^{k})
.
Using Proposition
23
and properties of
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}
ensured in its definition, we obtain the required inequalities:

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{k})+\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\leq f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}\Big(\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}\Big(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{v}^{k})+\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{i})\Big)=f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime}))\,,

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}))

\displaystyle=f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}\big(\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k+1}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k})\big)\big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{pow}\big(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{n-1}),\dots,\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{k+1}),\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{v}^{k})\big)=\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})\,.

∎

Proof 7.21

(Proof of Lemma
33
)
.

The proof is by induction on the length of the
r
-upper run
R
.
Proposition
9
gives us possible forms of
R
; we analyze these cases.

If
R
has length
0
, then we can take
\tau
as
\sigma
and the identity function as
f_{R}
;
given
\mathbf{s}
we take it as
\mathbf{t}
, and as
S
we take the run of length
0
from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
.

Suppose that
R
has length
1
, and its transition either is
\mathsf{read}
or performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
.
Then we construct
\sigma
and
\mathbf{v}^{0}
out of
R
and
\tau
as in Lemma
29
or Lemma
31
, respectively.
Recall that
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
, as needed.
As
f_{R}
we take the function
f_{\mathbf{v}^{0}}
constructed in Lemma
34
for the annotated
0
-stack
\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
Next, we are given a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))=\{\sigma\}

and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}))\cong\mathsf{top}^{r}(R(0))
.
Let
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
be the annotated
n
-stack obtained from
\mathbf{s}
by replacing its topmost
0
-stack with
\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
Because
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{v}^{0})=\{\sigma\}=\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))
, we have that
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is also well-formed.
As
\mathbf{t}
we take the successor of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, and as
S
the one-step run from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})
(i.e., from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
) to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
.
By Property
(\star)
of Lemma
29
or Lemma
31
, respectively,
the successor of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
indeed exists, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{t}))=\{\tau\}
;
moreover, the run
S
performs the same transition as
R
, and in the case of
\mathsf{read}
it reads the same letter, so
S
is
(r,\varphi)
-parallel to
R
.
Recalling that
f_{R}
satisfies the thesis of Lemma
34
, and using Lemma
18
,
we obtain the required inequalities:

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})\leq f_{R}(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime}))\leq f_{R}(\sharp(S)+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}))\,,

and

\displaystyle f_{R}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}))\geq\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})\geq\sharp(S)+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t})\,.

Next, suppose that
R
has length
1
and performs
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
for
k\leq r
.
We construct
\sigma
and
\mathbf{v}^{k}
out of
R
and
\tau
as in Lemma
30
.
Recall that
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
, as needed.
As
f_{R}
we take the function
f_{\mathbf{v}^{k}}
constructed in Lemma
34
for the annotated
k
-stack
\mathbf{v}^{k}
.
Then, we are given an annotated stack
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{s}^{k}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))=\{\sigma\}
and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}))\cong\mathsf{top}^{r}(R(0))
.
Consider
