\mathbf{s}^{\prime}=\mathbf{s}^{n}:\mathbf{s}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{s}^{k+1}:\mathbf{v}^{k}
.
Because
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{v}^{k}))=\{\sigma\}=\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))
,
by Proposition
15
we have that
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{v}^{k})=\{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\sigma)\}=\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{k}(\mathbf{s}))
;
in effect
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is also well-formed.
As
\mathbf{t}
we take the successor of
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, and as
S
the one-step run from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})
to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
.
By Property
(\star)
of Lemma
30
, the successor of
\mathbf{s}
indeed exists, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{t}))=\{\tau\}
,
and the transition of
S
performs
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
.
Clearly
S
is
(r,\varphi)
-parallel to
R
.
The required inequalities are obtained in the same way as in the previous case, due to Lemmas
34
and
18
.

Next, suppose that
R{\restriction}_{0,1}
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
and
R{\restriction}_{1,|R|}
is a
k
-return, where
k\geq r+1
.
This case is similar, but slightly more complicated.
First, using Lemma
28
, we construct a run descriptor
\rho\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(1))
such that
(\varphi(R),\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\in\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\rho)
.
Then, we construct
\sigma
and
\mathbf{v}^{0}
out of
R
,
\tau
, and
\rho
as in Lemma
32
.
Recall that
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
, as needed.
As
f_{R}
we take the function
f_{\mathbf{v}^{0}}
constructed in Lemma
34
for the annotated
0
-stack
\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
When we are given
\mathbf{s}
, we proceed as follows.
First, as
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
we take the annotated
n
-stack obtained from
\mathbf{s}
by replacing its topmost
0
-stack with
\mathbf{v}^{0}
.
As in the previous cases,
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is well-formed.
Let
\mathbf{S}
be the one-step annotated run from
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, and let
\mathbf{S}(1)=\mathbf{u}^{n}:\mathbf{u}^{n-1}:\dots:\mathbf{u}^{0}
.
By Property
(\star)
of Lemma
32
,
\mathbf{S}
indeed exists,
and
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S})
performs
\mathsf{push}^{k}_{\alpha}
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{u}^{0})=\{\rho\}
, and
\tau\in\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{u}^{k}))
.
Because
(\varphi(R),\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau))\in\mathsf{ass}^{k}(\rho)
,
Lemma
27
gives us an annotated run
\mathbf{T}
starting in
\mathbf{S}(1)
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{T})
is a
k
-return,

\varphi(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{T}))=\varphi(R)
, and
\mathsf{top}^{k}(\mathbf{T}(|\mathbf{T}|))=\mathbf{u}^{k}{\restriction}_{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)}
.
As
S
we take
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S}\circ\mathbf{T})
, and as
\mathbf{t}
we take
\mathbf{T}(|\mathbf{T}|)
.
Proposition
24
implies that
\{\tau\}=\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{u}^{k}{\restriction}_{\mathsf{red}^{k}(\tau)}))=\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{t}))
.
By Proposition
8

we obtain that
\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(0))\cong\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(|R|))

and
\mathsf{top}^{k}(S(0))\cong\mathsf{top}^{k}(S(|S|))
.
Since
k\geq r+1
,
\mathsf{top}^{r}(R(|R|))\cong\mathsf{top}^{r}(S(|S|))
as well.
Together with
\varphi(S)=\varphi(S{\restriction}_{1,|S|})=\varphi(R{\restriction}_{1,|R|})=\varphi(R)
this means that
R
and
S
are
(r,\varphi)
-parallel,
because by definition no suffix of a
k
-return can be
(k-1)
-upper (
r
-upper).
The inequalities are obtained as in the previous cases.

Finally, suppose that
R
is a composition of shorter
k
-upper runs
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
.
The induction assumption used for
R_{2}
and for
\tau
gives us a run descriptor
\rho\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R_{2}(0))
and a function
f_{2}
.
Then, the induction assumption used for
R_{1}
and for
\rho
gives us a run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
and a function
f_{1}
.
As
f_{R}
we take a monotone function such that for each pair
a
,
b
of natural numbers it holds
f_{R}(a)\geq f_{1}(a)+f_{2}(f_{1}(a))
and
f_{R}(a+b)\geq f_{1}(a+f_{2}(b))
.

Then, we are given a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}))=\{\sigma\}

and
\mathsf{top}^{r}(\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}))\cong\mathsf{top}^{r}(R(0))
.
From the induction assumption for
R_{1}
we obtain a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{u}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{u}))=\{\rho\}
,
and a run
S_{1}
from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s})
to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{u})
being
(r,\varphi)
-parallel to
R_{1}
.
Then, from the induction assumption for
R_{2}
we obtain a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{t}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{t}))=\{\tau\}
,
and a run
S_{2}
from
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{u})
to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t})
being
(r,\varphi)
-parallel to
R_{2}
.
As
S
we take the composition of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
; it is
(r,\varphi)
-parallel to
R
.
Using the inequalities from the induction assumption we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle\leq f_{1}(\sharp(S_{1})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{u}))\leq f_{1}(\sharp(S_{1})+f_{2}(\sharp(S_{2})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t})))

\displaystyle\leq f_{R}(\sharp(S_{1})+\sharp(S_{2})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}))=f_{R}(\sharp(S)+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}))\,

\displaystyle f_{R}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}))

\displaystyle\geq f_{1}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}))+f_{2}(f_{1}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}))\geq\sharp(S_{1})+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{u})+f_{2}(\sharp(S_{1})+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{u}))

\displaystyle\geq\sharp(S_{1})+f_{2}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{u}))\geq\sharp(S_{1})+\sharp(S_{2})+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t})=\sharp(S)+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t})\,.

∎

7.7.
Sequence-Equivalence

In the final part of this section we define sequence-equivalence, and we prove Theorem
12
.

{defi}

Let
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of configurations.
We define
\mathsf{stype}\!\left((c_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\right)\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}^{0}
to be the set of such
\sigma\in{\mathcal{T}}^{0}
that
there exists a sequence of well-formed annotated
n
-stacks
(\mathbf{s}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
for which
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))=\{\sigma\}
and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}_{i})=c_{i}
for each
i
,
and the sequence
(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is bounded
(notice that we require the same type
\{\sigma\}
for all
i
).
We say that two sequences of configurations,
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
are
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-sequence-equivalent
when it holds that

\mathsf{stype}\!\left((c_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\right)=\mathsf{stype}\!\left((d_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\right)
.

Proof 7.22

(Proof of Theorem
12
)
.

Recall that we are given a run
R\circ R^{\prime}
in which
R
is
k
-upper and
R^{\prime}
is an
n
-return;
we are also given two infinite sequences of configurations
c_{1},c_{2},\dots
and
d_{1},d_{2},\dots
that are
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-sequence-equivalent, and
in which all configurations have the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same positionless topmost
k
-stack as
R(0)
.
Our goal is to construct, for each
i
, runs
S_{i}\circ S_{i}^{\prime}
from
c_{i}
, and
T_{i}\circ T_{i}^{\prime}
from
d_{i}
in which
S_{i}
and
T_{i}
are
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
R
,
and
S_{i}^{\prime}
and
T_{i}^{\prime}
are
n
-returns such that
\varphi(S_{i}^{\prime})=\varphi(T_{i}^{\prime})=\varphi(R^{\prime})
, and such that
the sequences
\sharp(S_{1}\circ S_{1}^{\prime}),\sharp(S_{2}\circ S_{2}^{\prime}),\dots
and
\sharp(T_{1}\circ T_{1}^{\prime}),\sharp(T_{2}\circ T_{2}^{\prime}),\dots
are either both bounded or both unbounded.
Let
\xi=(\pi_{1}(R^{\prime}(|R^{\prime}|)),\emptyset,\dots,\emptyset,\mathsf{np})
. We see that
\xi\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R^{\prime}(|R^{\prime}|))
,
because we can annotate the topmost
0
-stack
(\gamma,x)
by
\{(\mathsf{empty},\gamma,\pi_{1}(R^{\prime}(|R^{\prime}|)))\}
and all other
0
-stacks by
\emptyset
.
Lemma
28
applied to
R^{\prime}
and
\xi
implies that
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R^{\prime}(0))
contains a run descriptor
\tau
such that
(\varphi(R^{\prime}),\mathsf{red}^{n}(\xi))\in\mathsf{ass}^{n}(\tau)
.
Then, Lemma
33
applied to
R
and
\tau
gives us a run descriptor
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))
and a function
f_{R}
.
We have two cases.

Case 1

Suppose first that
\sigma\in\mathsf{stype}\!\left((c_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\right)
(hence also
\sigma\in\mathsf{stype}\!\left((d_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\right)
).
Then we have a sequence of annotated
n
-stacks
(\mathbf{s}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))=\{\sigma\}
and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}_{i})=c_{i}
for each
i
,
and the sequence
(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is bounded.
Recall that the topmost
k
-stacks of
c_{i}
and of
R(0)
are positionless-equal, for each
i
.
We use Property
(\star)
of Lemma
33
for the annotated stack
\mathbf{s}_{i}
.
We obtain a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{t}_{i}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{t}_{i}))=\{\tau\}
, and a run
S_{i}
from
c_{i}
to
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{t}_{i})
being
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
R
and such that

f_{R}(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))\geq\sharp(S_{i})+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}_{i})
.
Next, for each
i
we apply Lemma
27
for
\mathbf{t}_{i}
and for the pair
(\varphi(R^{\prime}),\mathsf{red}^{n}(\xi))
.
We obtain an annotated run
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{i}
starting in
\mathbf{t}_{i}
such that
\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{i})
is an
n
-return,
\varphi(\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{i}))=\varphi(R^{\prime})
, and
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{i}(|\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{i}|))=\{\mathsf{red}^{n}(\xi)\}
.
Let
S^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{st}(\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{i})
, and
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{i}(|\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{i}|)
.
Thanks to Lemma
18
,
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{t}_{i})\geq\sharp(S^{\prime}_{i})+\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{u}_{i})
.
Because
(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is bounded, we see that the sequence
(\sharp(S_{i}\circ S^{\prime}_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is bounded as well.

We perform the same construction for
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, obtaining runs
T_{i}\circ T^{\prime}_{i}
from
d_{i}
, such that
(\sharp(T_{i}\circ T^{\prime}_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is bounded.

Case 2

This is the opposite case: we suppose that
\sigma\not\in\mathsf{stype}\!\left((c_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\right)
.
Recall that
\sigma\in\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(R(0))=\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\varphi}(c_{i})
for each
i
.
Using Proposition
26
we construct a well-formed annotated
n
-stack
\mathbf{s}_{i}
such that
\mathsf{type}(\mathsf{top}^{0}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))=\{\sigma\}
, and
\mathsf{conf}(\mathbf{s}_{i})=c_{i}
,
and
\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}_{i})\leq H(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))
for a function
H
not depending on
i
.
Our assumption ensures that
(\mathsf{high}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is unbounded, so
(\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}_{i}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is unbounded as well.
We construct the runs exactly in the same way as in Case 1, but this time we concentrate on the opposite inequalities.
For each
i
it holds that
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{s}_{i})\leq f_{R}(\sharp(S_{i})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{t}_{i}))\leq f_{R}(\sharp(S_{i}\circ S_{i}^{\prime})+\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{u}_{i}))
.
Additionally
\mathsf{low}(\mathbf{u}_{i})=0
, because
\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{u}_{i})=\mathsf{type}(\mathbf{S}_{i}^{\prime}(|\mathbf{S}_{i}^{\prime}|))=\{\mathsf{red}^{n}(\xi)\}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{np}}
(cf. Proposition
21
).
It follows that
(\sharp(S_{i}\circ S_{i}^{\prime}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
is unbounded, and similarly
(\sharp(T_{i}\circ T_{i}^{\prime}))_{i=1}^{\infty}
.
∎

8.
Milestone Configurations

In this section we define so-called milestone configurations and we show their basic properties.
The intuitions are as follows.
Consider a long run reading only stars.
Looking globally, the stack grows (or remains unchanged).
Locally, however, some parts of the stack might be constructed, and a few steps later removed.
In order to handle this behavior, we concentrate on those configurations of the run in which the stack is minimal (in appropriate sense) and will not be destroyed later;
they are called milestone configurations.

The idea of considering milestone configurations comes from Kartzow
[
Kar10
]
, but our definition is slightly different
(namely, their definition is relative to a run, which can be arbitrary, while our definition is absolute: we always consider the run reading only stars).
For this section we fix an
n
-DPDA
{\mathcal{A}}
with stack alphabet
\Gamma
and with input alphabet
A
containing a distinguished symbol denoted
\star
(star).

{defi}

We say that a configuration
c
is a
milestone
(or a milestone configuration) if there exists an infinite run
R
from
c
reading only stars,
and an infinite set
I
of indices such that
0\in I
, and
R{\restriction}_{i,j}\in\mathsf{up}^{0}
for all
i,j\in I
,
i\leq j
.

{exa}

Consider a DPDA of order 3.
Suppose that there is a run that begins in a stack
\mathsf{pos}_{+}([[[a,a]]])
, and performs forever the following sequence of operations, in a loop:

\displaystyle\mathsf{push}^{2}_{a}\,,\ \mathsf{push}^{3}_{a}\,,\ \mathsf{pop}^{1}\,,\ \mathsf{push}^{3}_{a}\,,\ \mathsf{pop}^{2}\,,\ \mathsf{push}^{3}_{a}\,.

Then the positionless topmost
2
-stack is, alternately,

[[a,a]]
, or
[[a,a],[a,a]]
, or
[[a,a],[a]]
.
This run does not read any symbols, so it is a degenerate case of an infinite run that reads only stars.
Configurations with positionless topmost
2
-stack
[[a,a]]
are milestones (and no other configurations in this run).
To obtain a less degenerate case, we may consider a loop of transitions as above, but containing additionally a
\mathsf{read}
transition;
when a star is read, the loop continues (we do not care what happens when any other symbol is read).
Then again configurations having
[[a,a]]
as the topmost
2
-stack are milestones.

If
c
is a milestone,
R
the (unique) infinite run from
c
reading only stars, and
I
a set like in the definition of a milestone,
then for each
i\in I
the configuration
R(i)
is a milestone as well.
The following lemma shows that in fact the set
I
can contain all indices
i
for which
R(i)
is a milestone.

Lemma 35
.

Let
R
be a run between two milestone configurations. If
R
reads only stars, then it is
0
-upper.

Proof 8.1
.

We prove by induction on
n-k
, where
k\in[0,n]
, that each run
R
as in the lemma is
k
-upper.
Trivially each run is
n
-upper.
Now suppose that the thesis holds for some
k>0
, take a run
R
between two milestone configurations, and suppose that it reads only stars.
Let
S
be the infinite run that starts in
R(0)
and reads only stars (since
R(0)
is a milestone, the run is really infinite);
R
is its prefix.
Notice that we can find a milestone
S(i)
such that
i\geq|R|
and
S{\restriction}_{0,i}
is
(k-1)
-upper (it can be even
0
-upper):
it is enough to take any
i\geq|R|
from the infinite set
I
from Definition
8
.
From the induction assumption we know that
S{\restriction}_{|R|,i}
is
k
-upper.
We conclude that
S{\restriction}_{0,|R|}=R
is
(k-1)
-upper using Proposition
5
for the run
S{\restriction}_{0,|R|}\circ S{\restriction}_{|R|,i}
.
∎

Another important property is that in a very long run reading only stars we can find a milestone configuration.
What “very long” means of course depends on the size of the configuration where the run starts.

Lemma 36
.

Let
l\in[1,n]
.
There exists a function
\beta
, assigning a natural number to every positionless
l
-stack, having the following property.
Let
R
be a run that reads only stars, let
s^{l}_{|R|}
be an
l
-stack of
R(|R|)
,
and let
s^{l}_{i}=\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{i,|R|},s^{l}_{|R|})
for all
i\in[0,|R|]
.
If there exist at least
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{l}_{0}))
indices
i
such that

s^{l}_{i}=\mathsf{top}^{l}(R(i))
,
then for some index
i
the configuration
R(i)
is a milestone and
s^{l}_{i}=\mathsf{top}^{l}(R(i))
.

Corollary 37
.

If
R
is an infinite run reading only stars,
then for infinitely many indices
i
the configuration
R(i)
is a milestone.

Proof 8.2
.

To obtain a first milestone configuration,
it is enough to use Lemma
36
for
l:=n
and for the prefix of
R
of length
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(\pi_{2}(R(0))))
.
We repeat this procedure for the remaining suffix of
R
.
4
4

4

There exists a direct proof of this corollary, not presented here, which is much easier than the proof of Lemma
36
.

∎

In order to get some intuitions on Lemma
36
, let us first see why it works for
l=n
.
In this case
s^{l}_{0}
is just the whole
n
-stack of
R(0)
.
Moreover, the assumption that there exist at least
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{l}_{0}))
indices
i
such that
s^{l}_{i}=\mathsf{top}^{l}(R(i))

simply expresses that
|R|+1\geq\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{l}_{0}))
.
Thus, the lemma says that if we have a long enough run that starts in a configuration with stack
s^{l}_{0}
and reads only stars, then the run reaches a milestone configuration.
This, in turn, means that we cannot decrease the stack
s^{l}_{0}
forever.
Indeed, recall the intuition that a milestone configuration is a minimal configuration, that is, such that the run reading only stars never visits a “smaller” configuration.
It is just enough to consider the infinite run reading only stars, and take the ,,smallest” configuration visited by this run; this should be a milestone configuration.

When
l<n
, the lemma concentrates on the history of a single
l
-stack
s^{l}_{|R|}

(another point of view is that it concentrates on the future of a single
l
-stack
s^{l}_{0}
).
We look at the fragments of
R
where this
l
-stack is the topmost
l
-stack;
the length of these fragments is required to be at least
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{l}_{0}))
in total.
The lemma says that regardless of what happens in other fragments of
R
,
controlled by other parts of the
n
-stack of
R(0)
(outside of
s^{l}_{0}
),
the stack
s^{l}_{0}
can itself ensure that a milestone configuration is reached.

In the remaining part of the section we prove Lemma
36
.
Our proof strategy is as follows.
The indices
i
for which
s^{l}_{i}
is the topmost
l
-stack give us a decomposition of an infix of
R
into many
l
-upper runs.
As a first step, consecutively for
k=l-1,l-2,\dots,0
we construct a decomposition of an infix of
R
into many
k
-upper runs.
Then, among the borders of the constructed
0
-upper runs we find two configurations having the same type.
Using Theorem
11
we can replicate the
0
-upper run between them into arbitrarily many consecutive
0
-upper runs,
proving that these two configurations are milestones.

The division of an infix of
R
into
k
-upper runs is described using
k
-advancing sets, defined as follows.
Assuming that
R
is fixed, a set
I^{k}\subseteq[0,|R|]
is called
k
-advancing
if

\displaystyle\emptyset\neq I^{k}=\{i\in[\min I^{k},\max I^{k}]\colon\,R{\restriction}_{i,\max I^{k}}\in\mathsf{up}^{k}\}\,.

Notice that when
\min I^{k}\leq i\leq j\in I^{k}
, then
i
belongs to
I^{k}
if and only if
R{\restriction}_{i,j}
is
k
-upper.
In other words, a
k
-advancing set not only gives us a decomposition into
k
-upper runs, but also these
k
-upper runs cannot be further subdivided into shorter
k
-upper runs.
The following auxiliary lemma describes our induction step.

Lemma 38
.

Let
k\in[1,n]
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
There exists a function
f^{k}_{N}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, having the following properties.
Let
R
be a run that reads only stars, and let
I^{k}
be a
k
-advancing set.
If
|I^{k}|\geq f^{k}_{N}(|\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(\min I^{k}))|)
,
then there exists a
(k-1)
-advancing subset
I^{k-1}\subseteq I^{k}
of size at least
N
, such that

\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(\mathsf{top}^{k-1}(R(\min I^{k-1})))
is one of the
(k-1)
-stacks in
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(\min I^{k})))
.

Proof 8.3
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
N
.
For
N=1
we can take
f^{k}_{1}(r):=1
, and then
I^{k-1}:=\{\min I^{k}\}
.
Let now
N\geq 2
.
We take

\displaystyle f^{k}_{N}(r):=1+\sum_{m=1}^{r}f^{k}_{N-1}(m+1)\,.

Fix some
R
and
I^{k}
satisfying the assumptions.
Let
a:=\min I^{k}
and
r:=|\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(\min I^{k}))|
.
For each
j\in I^{k}
denote

\displaystyle r_{j}:=|\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(j))|

and

\displaystyle m_{j}:=\min\{r_{i}\colon i\in I^{k}\land i\leq j\}\,.

Notice that
1\leq m_{j}\leq r
(because
r_{a}=r
) and that
m_{j}\geq m_{j^{\prime}}
for
j\leq j^{\prime}
.
From the formula for
f^{k}_{N}(r)
we see that for some
m
we have at least
f^{k}_{N-1}(m+1)+1
indices
j\in I^{k}
such that
m_{j}=m
,
by the pigeonhole principle (if for every
m
there were at most
f^{k}_{N-1}(m+1)
such indices,
in total we would have at most
\sum_{m=1}^{r}f^{k}_{N-1}(m+1)=f^{k}_{N}(r)-1
indices in
I^{k}
, but we have at least
f^{k}_{N}(r)
of them).
Choose some such
m
; let
b
be the first index such that
m_{b}=m
, and
e
the last such index.
We see that
m=r_{b}
.

Let
c
be the next element of
I^{k}
after
b
(of course
c\leq e
).
Notice that
r_{c}\leq r_{b}+1=m+1
; this follows from Proposition
3
used for the run
R{\restriction}_{b,c}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle|I^{k}\cap[c,e]|\geq f^{k}_{N-1}(m+1)\geq f^{k}_{N-1}(r_{c})\,.

We use the induction assumption for
I^{k}\cap[c,e]
.
We obtain a
(k-1)
-advancing subset
J^{k-1}\subseteq I^{k}\cap[c,e]
of size at least
N-1
.
We take

\displaystyle I^{k-1}:=\{i\in[b,\min J^{k-1}]\colon\,R{\restriction}_{i,\max J^{k-1}}\in\mathsf{up}^{k-1}\}\cup J^{k-1}\,.

We easily see that
I^{k-1}
is
(k-1)
-advancing (we add to
J^{k-1}
exactly these indices for which the appropriate run is
(k-1)
-upper).
Because
r_{i}\geq m_{i}=m=r_{b}
for each
i\in I^{k}\cap[b,e]
(hence, in particular, for each
i\in I^{k}\cap[b,\max J^{k-1}]
),
Proposition
4
implies that
R{\restriction}_{b,\max J^{k-1}}
is
(k-1)
-upper.
Thus,
I^{k-1}
in addition to the
N-1
elements of
J^{k-1}
contains at least one additional element
b
.

Finally, we show that
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(\mathsf{top}^{k-1}(R(b)))

is one of the
(k-1)
-stacks in
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(a)))
.
We know that
r_{i}\geq m_{i}>m=r_{b}
for each
i\in I^{k}\cap[a,b-1]
, so, due to Proposition
4
, run
R{\restriction}_{i,b}
is not
(k-1)
-upper.
This means that the topmost
(k-1)
-stack of
R(b)
was not modified since
R(a)
.
On the other hand
R{\restriction}_{a,b}
is
k
-upper,
thus, indeed, the topmost
(k-1)
-stack of
R(b)
is one of the
(k-1)
-stacks in the topmost
k
-stack of
R(a)
(while ignoring positions annotating the stacks).
∎

While using Theorem
11
we need to ensure that the replicated run reads only stars.
For this reason, fix a finite monoid
M
, and a morphism
\varphi\colon A^{*}\to M
,
such that its value
\varphi(w)
determines whether a word
w
consists only of stars.
Our second auxiliary lemma is used to conclude the proof of Lemma
36
.

Lemma 39
.

Let
S
be a nonempty
0
-upper run reading only stars, in which
S(|S|)
has the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same topmost stack symbol as
S(0)
(where
\varphi
as above).
Then
S
can be extended into a run
S\circ T\circ U
reading only stars, where
T
and
U
are nonempty
0
-upper runs,
and
U(|U|)
has the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same topmost stack symbol as
U(0)
.
As a consequence,
S(0)
is a milestone.

Proof 8.4
.

First, we observe that for each
r\in\mathbb{N}
we can construct a composition
S_{1}\circ\dots\circ S_{r}
of
r
nonempty
0
-upper runs, reading only stars, in which
S_{1}=S
.
For
r=1
this is trivially true.
Suppose that we have such a composition for some
r
.
Then Theorem
11
applied to this composition and to
S(|S|)

(where we use the fact that
S(|S|)
has the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same positionless topmost
0
-stack as
(S_{1}\circ\dots\circ S_{r})(0)
)
gives us a run that starts in
S(|S|)
and is
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-parallel to
S_{1}\circ\dots\circ S_{r}
.
Recalling the definition of being
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-parallel, we see that this run is a composition
S^{\prime}_{1}\circ\dots\circ S^{\prime}_{r}

of
r
nonempty
0
-upper runs reading only stars.
Together with
S
at the beginning, they give a longer composition as required.

Take such a composition for
r
equal to the number of stack symbols in our alphabet
\Gamma
, times the number of
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-types, plus two.
Then, by the pigeonhole principle, we can find two indices
i,j\in[2,r]
with
i<j
for which
S_{j}(|S_{j}|)
has the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same topmost stack symbol as
S_{i}(|S_{i}|)
.
Skipping the part after
S_{j}
, we obtain a composition
S\circ T\circ U
as required.

We can repeat the same construction for
U
, and append two more nonempty
0
-upper runs, out of which the second has equal
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and topmost stack symbol at its two ends.
Continuing this forever, we obtain an infinite run reading only stars, divided into
0
-upper runs.
Since it starts in
S(0)
, this configuration is a milestone.
∎

Proof 8.5

(Proof of Lemma
36
)
.

Fix some
l
-stack
s^{l}_{0}
.
Let
N_{0}
be equal to the number of stack symbols in the alphabet, times the number of
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-types, plus one, where again
\varphi
checks whether a word consists only of stars.
For
k\in[1,l]
we take
N_{k}=f^{k}_{N_{k-1}}(r_{k})
, where
r_{k}
is the maximal size of a
k
-stack that appears in
s^{l}_{0}
,
and
f^{k}_{N_{k-1}}
is the function from Lemma
38
.
We define
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{l}_{0})):=N_{l}
.

Now take a run
R
and
l
-stacks
s^{l}_{i}
for
i\in[1,|R|]
, such that the assumptions of the lemma are satisfied.
First, for each
k\in[0,l]
we want to construct a
k
-advancing set
I^{k}
of size at least
N_{k}
,
such that
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(\min I^{k})))
is one of the
k
-stacks in
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{l}_{0})
.

As
I^{l}
we take the set of those indices
i
for which
s^{l}_{i}=\mathsf{top}^{l}(R(i))
.
It is immediate from the definitions that
I^{l}
is
l
-advancing
(recall that
s^{l}_{i}=\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{i,j},s^{l}_{j})
for
i\leq j
).
By assumption
|I^{l}|\geq\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{l}_{0}))=N_{l}
.
Moreover,
s^{l}_{\min I^{l}}
was not modified from the beginning of the run (as it was not the topmost
l
-stack),
so this
l
-stack is positionless-equal to
s^{l}_{0}
.

Then by induction on
l-k
, we construct
I^{k-1}
out of
I^{k}
using Lemma
38
.
Notice that the size of the topmost
k
-stack of
R(\min I^{k})
is at most
r_{k}
, so we can indeed obtain
I^{k-1}
of size at least
N_{k-1}
.

Finally, we have a
0
-advancing set
I^{0}
such that
|I^{0}|\geq N_{0}
.
Observe that in
I^{0}
we can find two indices
i,j
with
i<j
such that
R(j)
has the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same topmost stack symbol as
R(i)
,
by the pigeonhole principle (recall the definition of
N_{0}
from the first paragraph of the proof).
Lemma
39
applied to
R{\restriction}_{i,j}
proves that
R(i)
is a milestone.
By construction
i\in I^{0}\subseteq\dots\subseteq I^{l}
, so
s^{l}_{i}=\mathsf{top}^{l}(R(i))
.
∎

9.
Pumping Lemma

In this section we present a pumping lemma, which can be used to change the number of stars read in some place of a run, without changing too much the rest of the run.
For this section we fix an
n
-DPDA
{\mathcal{A}}
with input alphabet
A
containing a
\star
symbol.
We also fix a morphism
\varphi\colon A^{*}\to M
into a finite monoid.

Figure 5
.
An example configuration at the end of a run of a
2
-DPDA, and an analogous configuration after pumping.
The
2
-stack grows from left to right.
White symbols were already present in
R(0)
;
dark gray symbols were created while reading stars at the beginning of
R
;
light gray symbols were created later.

We start by an intuitive explanation of the pumping lemma.
In the situation that we consider, we have a milestone configuration from which we start runs that first read some number of stars, and later also other symbols.
One possibility is that most of these runs are
k
-upper (for some
k
), except maybe some runs reading a small number of stars at the beginning.
Then we are unable to use our pumping lemma, but we gain the knowledge that our run is
k
-upper.
The opposite situation is that there are runs from this configuration that are not
k
-upper and read arbitrarily many stars at the beginning; our pumping lemma talks about this situation.
Consider such a run
R
of a
2
-DPDA, whose last configuration is depicted on the left of Figure
5
.
It starts in a milestone, so its initial fragment that reads only stars is basically
0
-upper.
This means that the automaton builds on top of the stack of
R(0)
(depicted in white), without modifying it;
also in the copies of the topmost
1
-stack the original part is not modified (the automaton can inspect this part, but then it has to be removed).
We consider a run that is not
0
-upper, so later, when we start reading other symbols than stars, the “white part” of the topmost
1
-stack is uncovered;
its content is the same as in
R(0)
.
By assumption there exists a run from the same configuration that reads more (arbitrarily many) stars at the beginning, and is not
0
-upper.
When it uncovers the “white part” of the topmost
1
-stack, this part is exactly the same as in the original run, so these runs can continue in the same way.
This is depicted on the right of the figure.

Next, we state our pumping lemma.
For uniformity of presentation, we refer there to
(-1)
-upper runs, with the assumption that no run is
(-1)
-upper.

Theorem 40

(Pumping lemma)
.

For each milestone configuration
c
there exists a number
\mathsf{pb}(c)
having the following property.
Let
R\circ R^{\prime}
be a run starting in
c
, where
R
is not
(k-1)
-upper and reads a word beginning with at least
\mathsf{pb}(c)
stars, and
R^{\prime}
is
k
-upper.
In such a situation, for each
l\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a run
S\circ S^{\prime}
starting in
c
,
and such that
\varphi(S)=\varphi(R)
, and
S
reads a word beginning with at least
l
stars, and
S^{\prime}
is
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
R^{\prime}
.

Let us mention that another pumping lemma for higher-order pushdown automata was presented in a former paper by the author
[
Par12c
]
.
There are several differences between these two lemmas.
An advantage of the former lemma is that it gives a precise value for
\mathsf{pb}(c)
, in terms of the size of
c
.
Moreover, it works not only for deterministic PDA, but also for nondeterministic PDA in which the
\varepsilon
-closure of the configuration graph is finitely-branching.
On the other hand, the former pumping lemma is only given for
k=0
.
Additionally, it just says that the length of the word read by the run increases, not necessarily the number of stars at its beginning.
The former pumping lemma was later generalized to collapsible pushdown automata
[
KP12
]
.

In the rest of the section we present a proof of Theorem
40
.
Its essence is as described above: we consider the moment when the run ceases to be
(k-1)
-upper.
One possibility is as depicted in Figure
5
: this happens during a
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
operation;
our topmost
k
-stack becomes positionless-equal to
\mathsf{pop}^{k}(\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(0)))
.
This can also happen during a
\mathsf{pop}^{r}
operation for some
r>k
.
Then we can obtain another topmost
k
-stack, but altogether we have only finitely many possibilities.
At least one of these possibilities happens for runs reading arbitrarily large number of stars at the beginning, by the pigeonhole principle; we can stick to this possibility.
Next, when we change the number of stars read at the beginning, we still land in a configuration having the same positionless topmost
k
-stack as in the original run,
when the run ceases to be
(k-1)
-upper;
from this configuration we can mimic the rest of the original run.
When
k<n-1
, the type of the rest of the stack is important as well
(the latter fragment of the run can perform returns visiting interiors of our stack; the existence of such returns is described by the type).
This is not a problem, since the type comes from a finite set, so we can assume that it is fixed as well.

The most difficult part of the proof is to show that indeed when the run ceases to be
(k-1)
-upper, there are only finitely many possible shapes for the topmost
k
-stack.
This is shown in Corollary
42
.
It is based on Lemma
41
, in which we analyze the situation just after reading the stars.

In order to state Lemma
41
, we need two definitions.
For a run
R
starting in a configuration
c
, and for a
k
-stack
s^{k}
in some configuration
R(i)
, where
k\in[1,n]
,
we say that
s^{k}
is
c
-clear
in
R(i)
(with respect to
R
) when
\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{0,i},\mathsf{top}^{k-1}(s^{k}))\neq\mathsf{top}^{k-1}(c)
.
Moreover, for a configuration
c
, and for
k\in[1,n]
, let
{\mathcal{S}}^{k}(c)
be the smallest set of positionless
k
-stacks such that
if
R
is a run that starts in
c
and reads only stars, and
s^{k}
is a
k
-stack of
R(|R|)
that is
c
-clear with respect to
R
, then
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(s^{k})\in{\mathcal{S}}^{k}(c)
.

Lemma 41
.

For each milestone configuration
c
, and for
k\in[1,n]
, the set
{\mathcal{S}}^{k}(c)
is finite.

Proof 9.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}(c)
be the set containing all positionless
k
-stacks of
c
,
and additionally
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(\mathsf{pop}^{k}(\mathsf{top}^{k}(c)))
; clearly
{\mathcal{X}}(c)
is finite.
We claim that every positionless
k
-stack in
{\mathcal{S}}^{k}(c)
can be obtained from a positionless
k
-stack
s^{k}_{-}\in{\mathcal{X}}(c)
by applying at most
\beta(s^{k}_{-})

\mathsf{push}
and
\mathsf{pop}
operations, where
\beta
is the function from Lemma
36
;
this immediately implies that
{\mathcal{S}}^{k}(c)
is finite.

Fix a run
R
that starts in
c
and reads only stars, and fix a
c
-clear
k
-stack
s^{k}
of
R(|R|)
.
Consider the smallest index
i
for which the
k
-stack
\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{i,|R|},s^{k})
is
c
-clear in
R(i)
; denote
t^{k}=\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{i,|R|},s^{k})
.
We claim that
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(t^{k})\in{\mathcal{X}}(c)
.
Indeed, either
i=0
and
t^{k}
is one of the
k
-stacks of
c
,
or
\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{i-1,|R|},s^{k})
is not
c
-clear in
R(i-1)
.
In the latter case, this
k
-stack becomes
c
-clear in the next configuration, so necessarily this is the topmost
k
-stack,
and the operation between these configurations is
\mathsf{pop}^{k}
.
We see that
R{\restriction}_{0,i-1}
is
(k-1)
-upper, and
R{\restriction}_{0,i}
is not
(k-1)
-upper.
Proposition
6
implies that
R{\restriction}_{0,i}
is a
k
-return, and Proposition
7
implies that

t^{k}=\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(i))\cong\mathsf{pop}^{k}(\mathsf{top}^{k}(c))
; thus,
\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(t^{k})\in{\mathcal{X}}(c)
.

Observe that
t^{k}
can be changed in
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
only when it is the topmost
k
-stack.
If there exist at most
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(t^{k}))
indices
j\in[i,|R|]

such that
\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{j,|R|},s^{k})=\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(j))
,
then
s^{k}
can be obtained from
t^{k}
by applying at most
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(t^{k}))

\mathsf{push}
and
\mathsf{pop}
operations, as we wanted to prove.

It remains to prove that indeed there are at most
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(t^{k}))
indices
j\in[i,|R|]
such that
\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{j,|R|},s^{k})=\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(j))
.
Suppose to the contrary that there are more than
\beta(\mathsf{pos}{\downarrow}(t^{k}))
such indices
j
.
Then we can use Lemma
36
for
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
;
it gives us an index
j
such that the configuration
R(j)
is a milestone and
\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{j,|R|},s^{k})=\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(j))
.
Because both
c
and
R(j)
are milestones, we know that
R{\restriction}_{0,j}
is
0
-upper, thanks to Lemma
35
.
One case is that
i=0
; then
\mathsf{hist}(R,s^{k})\neq\mathsf{top}^{k}(c)

(because, by the definition of
i
,
\mathsf{hist}(R,s^{k})
is
c
-clear in
c
),
and we know that
\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{j,|R|},s^{k})=\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(j))
,
so
R{\restriction}_{0,j}
is not
k
-upper; in particular it cannot be
0
-upper.
Otherwise, as already observed,
R{\restriction}_{0,i}
is not
(k-1)
-upper and
\mathsf{hist}(R{\restriction}_{i,|R|},s^{k})=\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(i))
, which implies that
R{\restriction}_{i,j}
is
k
-upper.
But
R{\restriction}_{0,j}
is
(k-1)
-upper, so this contradicts Proposition
5
applied for
R{\restriction}_{0,i}\circ R{\restriction}_{i,j}
.
∎

Corollary 42
.

For each milestone configuration
c
there exists a finite set
{\mathcal{S}}(c)
of configurations having the following property.
Let
k\in[0,n]
, let
R
be a run starting in
c
, and let
r\in[0,|R|]
be such that
R{\restriction}_{0,r}
reads only stars.
Suppose that
R
is not
(k-1)
-upper, but for each
i\in[r,|R|-1]
either
R{\restriction}_{0,i}
is
(k-1)
-upper or
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
is not
k
-upper.
Then we can find a configuration
d\in{\mathcal{S}}(c)
having the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same positionless topmost
k
-stack as
R(|R|)
.

Proof 9.2
.

There are only finitely many possible values of an
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type of a configuration.
Thus, it is enough to show, for each
k
, that there are only finitely many possible positionless topmost
k
-stacks over all configurations
R(|R|)
satisfying the assumptions.
For
k=0
this is trivial as a positionless
0
-stack contains just one symbol.
Suppose that
k\geq 1
.
We have two cases.

First suppose that
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
is
k
-upper for some
i\in[r,|R|-1]
; fix the greatest such index
i
.
Then by assumption
R{\restriction}_{0,i}
is
(k-1)
-upper, but
R
is not.
This is possible only when
i=|R|-1
(thanks to Proposition
3
used for
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
).
Proposition
6
says that
R
is necessarily a
k
-return.
Thus,
\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(|R|))\cong\mathsf{pop}^{k}(\mathsf{top}^{k}(R(0)))
(cf. Proposition
7
); the content of this
k
-stack is fixed.

The other case is that
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
is not
k
-upper for every
i\in[r,|R|-1]
.
This means that the topmost
k
-stack of
R(|R|)
is an unchanged copy of some
k
-stack of
R(r)
.
As
R
is not
(k-1)
-upper, this
k
-stack of
R(r)
is
c
-clear; it thus belongs to the set
{\mathcal{S}}^{k}(c)
, which is finite by Lemma
41
.
∎

Proof 9.3

(Proof of Theorem
40
)
.

Consider the infinite run
P
starting at the milestone configuration
c
and reading only stars.
Consider first the degenerate case when in
P
only finitely many stars are read.
As
\mathsf{pb}(c)
we take their number, plus one.
Then the thesis is satisfied trivially, as there is no run that starts in
c
and reads a word beginning with
\mathsf{pb}(c)
stars.
So for the rest of the proof suppose that
P
reads infinitely many stars.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}(c)
be the set from Corollary
42
(used for
c
).
For each
i\geq 1
we define the set
T_{i}\subseteq[0,n]\times{\mathcal{S}}(c)\times M
as follows.
A triple
(j,d,m)
belongs to
T_{i}
if and only if there exists a run
R
from
c

such that the word read by
R
begins with (at least)
i
stars, and
\varphi(R)=m
, and
R(|R|)
has the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same positionless topmost
j
-stack as
d
.
By definition
T_{i+1}\subseteq T_{i}
(for each
i
), and there are only finitely many possible sets, so from some moment every
T_{i}
is the same.
As
\mathsf{pb}(c)
we take a positive number such that
T_{i}=T_{\mathsf{pb}(c)}
for all
i\geq\mathsf{pb}(c)
.

Consider now a run
R\circ R^{\prime}
starting in
c
, where
R
is not
(k-1)
-upper and reads a word beginning with at least
\mathsf{pb}(c)
stars, and
R^{\prime}
is
k
-upper, for some
k\in[0,n]
.
Consider also a number
l
.
Our goal is to construct a run
S\circ S^{\prime}
starting in
c
and such that
\varphi(S)=\varphi(R)
, and
S
reads a word beginning with at least
l
stars, and
S^{\prime}
is
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
R^{\prime}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
l\geq\mathsf{pb}(c)
.
Let
r
be an index such that
R{\restriction}_{0,r}
reads exactly
\mathsf{pb}(c)
stars.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that there is no
i\in[r,|R|-1]
such that
R{\restriction}_{0,i}
is not
(k-1)
-upper and
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
is
k
-upper
(if such
i
exists, we move the subrun
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
to
R^{\prime}
, that is, we use the pumping lemma for
R{\restriction}_{0,i}\circ(R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}\circ R^{\prime})
,
and then in the resulting
S^{\prime}
we find the subrun
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
R{\restriction}_{i,|R|}
and we move it back to
S
).

We use Corollary
42
for
R
and
r
; its assumptions are satisfied thanks to our “without loss of generality” assumption.
We obtain some
d\in{\mathcal{S}}(c)
that has the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same positionless topmost
k
-stack as
R(|R|)
.
It means that
(k,d,\varphi(R))\in T_{\mathsf{pb}(c)}
.
Because
T_{\mathsf{pb}(c)}=T_{l}
, there exists a run
S
from
c
such that the word read by
S
begins with (at least)
l
stars, and
\varphi(S)=\varphi(R)
, and

S(|S|)
has the same
({\mathcal{A}},\varphi)
-type and the same topmost
k
-stack as
R(|R|)
.

Finally, we use Theorem
11
for
R^{\prime}
in order to obtain an accepting run
S^{\prime}
that starts in
S(|S|)
and is
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
R^{\prime}
.
∎

10.
Why
U
Cannot Be Recognized?

In this section we prove that the language
U
cannot be recognized by a deterministic higher-order pushdown automaton.
Notice that our techniques presented in previous sections were quite general (not too much related to the
U
language).
We believe that they can be useful for other purposes, for instance, to analyze behavior of some automata (in particular automata whose main objective is to count and compare
the number of times a symbol appears on the input).

Of course our proof is by contradiction: suppose that for some
n
we have an
(n-1)
-DPDA recognizing
U
.
We construct an
n
-DPDA
{\mathcal{A}}
that works as follows.
First it performs a
\mathsf{push}^{n}
operation.
Then it simulates the
(n-1)
-DPDA (not using the
\mathsf{push}^{n}
and
\mathsf{pop}^{n}
operations).
When the
(n-1)
-DPDA is going to accept,
{\mathcal{A}}
performs a
\mathsf{pop}^{n}
operation and afterwards accepts.
Clearly,
{\mathcal{A}}
recognizes
U
as well (here we use the fact that no word in
U
is a prefix of another word in
U
).
Such a normalization allows us to use Theorem
12
, as in
{\mathcal{A}}
every accepting run is an
n
-return.

Fix a finite monoid
M
and a morphism
\lambda\colon A^{*}\to M
that checks whether a word
is of the form
\sharp^{*}
(some number of
\sharp
symbols),
or of the form
\star^{*}]\star^{*}
(a closing bracket surrounded by some number of stars), or of neither of these two forms.
This means that
\lambda(u)\neq\lambda(v)
for all words
u,v
being of different forms.
Let
N
be the number of equivalence classes of the
({\mathcal{A}},\lambda)
-sequence-equivalence relation, times the number of
({\mathcal{A}},\lambda)
-types, plus one.
Consider the following words:

\displaystyle w_{0}=[\,,

\displaystyle w_{k+1}=w_{k}^{N}]^{N}[

\displaystyle\mbox{for }k\in[0,n-1]\,,

where the number in the superscript (in this case
N
) denotes the number of repetitions of a word.
For a word
w
, its
pattern
is a word obtained from
w
by removing its letters other than brackets (leaving only brackets).
Fix a morphism
\varphi\colon A^{*}\to M
such that from its value
\varphi(w)
we can deduce

•

whether the word
w
contains the
\sharp
symbol, and

•

whether the pattern of
w
is longer than
|w_{n}|
(recall that
n
is the order of
{\mathcal{A}}
), and

•

the exact value of the pattern of
w
, whenever this pattern is not longer than
|w_{n}|
.

We fix a run
R
, and an index
z(w)
for each prefix
w
of
w_{n}
, such that the following holds.
The run
R
begins in the initial configuration.
Between
R(0)
and
R(z(\varepsilon))
only stars are read.
For each prefix
w
of
w_{n}
, the configuration
R(z(w))
is a milestone.
Just after
z(w)
, the run
R
reads
\mathsf{pb}(R(z(w)))
stars, where
\mathsf{pb}
is the function from Theorem
40
used for morphism
\varphi
.
If
w=va
(where
a
is a single letter), the word read by
R
between
R(z(v))
and
R(z(w))
consists of
a
surrounded by some number of stars.
Of course such a run
R
exists: we read stars until we reach a milestone (succeeds thanks to Corollary
37
),
then we read as many stars as required by the pumping lemma, then we read the next letter of
w_{n}
, and so on (because
{\mathcal{A}}
accepts
U
, it will never block).

It is important to analyze relations between configurations
R(z(v))
for some prefixes
v
of
w_{n}
.
In order to avoid complicated subscripts, for any prefixes
v,w
of
w_{n}
we denote
\langle v,w\rangle:=R{\restriction}_{z(v),z(w)}
.

By construction of
{\mathcal{A}}
, for every prefix
v
of
w_{n}
the run
\langle v,w_{n}\rangle
is
(n-1)
-upper (as we never perform a
\mathsf{pop}^{n}
operation
before reading some
\sharp
symbol).
This contradicts the following key lemma (taken for
k=n-1
and
u=\varepsilon
).

Lemma 43
.

Let
k\in[-1,n-1]
, and let
u
be a word such that
uw_{k+1}
is a prefix of
w_{n}
.
Then there exist a prefix
v
of
w_{k+1}
such that
v\neq w_{k+1}
and

\langle uv,uw_{k+1}\rangle
is not
k
-upper.

Proof 10.1
.

The proof is by induction on
k
.
For
k=-1
this is obvious, as no run is
(-1)
-upper (we take
v=\varepsilon
).

Let now
k\geq 0
.
Figure
6
may be helpful in finding different runs present in the proof below.
Suppose that the thesis of the lemma does not hold.
Then for each prefix
v
of
w_{k+1}
the run
\langle uv,uw_{k+1}\rangle
is
k
-upper.
From this we get the following property
\heartsuit
.

Let
v^{\prime}
be a prefix of
w_{k+1}
, and
v
a prefix of
v^{\prime}
.
Then
\langle uv,uv^{\prime}\rangle
is
k
-upper.

In the proof, we construct two sequences of accepting runs, with many extra stars inserted in two different places.
Namely, in one sequence we insert extra stars before the last opening bracket that is not closed, and in the other sequence—after this bracket.
In effect, the number of sharp symbols read by runs in one of these sequences should be unbounded, and in the other—bounded.
The sequences are constructed in such a way that this violates Theorem
12
,
which says that the number of sharp symbols read by runs in these sequences is either bounded in both sequences or unbounded in both sequences.

Now we come to details.
By the induction assumption (where
uw_{k}^{i-1}
is taken as
u
), for each
i\in[1,N]
there exists a prefix
v_{i}
of
w_{k}
such that

\langle uw_{k}^{i-1}v_{i},uw_{k}^{i}\rangle
is not
(k-1)
-upper.
As
\langle uw_{k}^{i},uw_{k}^{N}\rangle
is
k
-upper (property
\heartsuit
), from Proposition
5
we know that
\langle uw_{k}^{i-1}v_{i},uw_{k}^{N}\rangle
cannot be
(k-1)
-upper as well.

Now we are ready to use the pumping lemma (Theorem
40
).
For each
i\in[1,N]
we use it for
\langle uw_{k}^{i-1}v_{i},uw_{k}^{N}\rangle\circ\langle uw_{k}^{N},uw_{k+1}\rangle
.
Recall from the definition of
R

that the word read by
\langle uw_{k}^{i-1}v_{i},uw_{k}^{N}\rangle
begins with such a number of stars that the pumping lemma can be used.
For each number
l
we obtain a run
S_{i,l}\circ S_{i,l}^{\prime}
, such that
\varphi(S_{i,l})=\varphi(\langle uw_{k}^{i-1}v_{i},uw_{k}^{N}\rangle)
, and
S_{i,l}
reads a word beginning with at least
l
stars,
and
S_{i,l}^{\prime}
is
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
\langle uw_{k}^{N},uw_{k+1}\rangle
; let
d_{i,l}=S_{i,l}(|S_{i,l}|)
.
Notice that the run
R{\restriction}_{0,z(uw_{k}^{i-1}v_{i})}\circ S_{i,l}
, starts in the initial configuration, ends in
d_{i,l}
, and reads a word having pattern
uw_{k}^{N}
.

Figure 6
.
Illustration of runs appearing in the proof (where
N=4
,
x=1
,
y=3
). Recall that stars can appear between letters of the words.

Because there are finitely many possible
({\mathcal{A}},\lambda)
-types,
we can assume that
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\lambda}(d_{i,l})=\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\lambda}(d_{i,j})
for each
i\in[1,N]
and each
l
and
j
.
Indeed, we can choose (for each
i
separately) some value of
\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\lambda}(d_{i,l})
that appears infinitely often,
and then we take the subsequence of only these
d_{i,l}
that give this value.

Since there are more possible indices
i\in[1,N]
than the number of classes of the
({\mathcal{A}},\lambda)
-sequence-equivalence relation,
times the number of
({\mathcal{A}},\lambda)
-types, there have to exist two indices
x
,
y
with
1\leq x<y\leq N
such that

\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\lambda}(d_{x,1})=\mathsf{type}_{{\mathcal{A}},\lambda}(d_{y,1})
, and the sequences

d_{x,1},d_{x,2},\dots
and
d_{y,1},d_{y,2},\dots
are
({\mathcal{A}},\lambda)
-sequence-equivalent.
From now we fix these two indices
x,y
.
Furthermore, because
S_{i,l}^{\prime}
is
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
\langle uw_{k}^{N},uw_{k+1}\rangle
for each
i\in[1,N]
and each
l
,
we know that the topmost
k
-stacks of all
d_{x,l}
and of all
d_{y,l}
are positionless-equal.

Let
R^{\prime}
be a prefix of
S_{x,1}^{\prime}
that is
(k,\varphi)
-parallel to
\langle uw_{k}^{N},uw_{k}^{N}]^{N-x}\rangle
.
Notice that
R^{\prime}
consists of
N-x
runs, each of which is
k
-upper and reads a word of the form
\star^{*}]\star^{*}
(a closing bracket surrounded by some number of stars).
Let also
R^{\prime\prime}
be an
n
-return that starts in
R^{\prime}(|R^{\prime}|)
and reads only
\sharp
symbols
(because
{\mathcal{A}}
recognizes
U
, there is an accepting run
R^{\prime\prime}
that starts in
R^{\prime}(|R^{\prime}|)
and reads only
\sharp
symbols;
by construction of
{\mathcal{A}}
, it is an
n
-return).

Finally, we use Theorem
12
for
\lambda
(as
\varphi
), sequences

d_{x,1},d_{x,2},\dots
(as
c_{1},c_{2},\dots
) and

d_{y,1},d_{y,2},\dots
(as
d_{1},d_{2},\dots
), and for run the
R^{\prime}\circ R^{\prime\prime}
.
5
5

5

Notice that we cannot use
\langle uw_{k}^{N},uw_{k}^{N}]^{N-x}\rangle
instead of
R^{\prime}
, because do not know anything about the
({\mathcal{A}},\lambda)
-type of
R(z(uw_{k}^{N})))
.

As noticed above (in particular because
R^{\prime}(0)=d_{x,1}
), the configurations
R^{\prime}(0)
, and
d_{x,l}
, and
d_{y,l}
for each
l
all have the same
({\mathcal{A}},\lambda)
-types and positionless topmost
k
-stacks.
Thus, the assumptions of the theorem are satisfied.
For each
l
, we obtain runs
S_{l}=S_{l}^{\prime}\circ S_{l}^{\prime\prime}
(from
d_{x,l}
) and
T_{l}=T_{l}^{\prime}\circ T_{l}^{\prime\prime}
(from
d_{y,l}
).
The word read by any of these runs contains
N-x
closing brackets with some number of stars around them,
and after them some number of
\sharp
symbols.

The runs
R{\restriction}_{0,z(uw_{k}^{x-1}v_{x})}\circ S_{x,l}\circ S_{l}
and
R{\restriction}_{0,z(uw_{k}^{y-1}v_{y})}\circ S_{y,l}\circ T_{l}
for each
l
have pattern
uw_{k}^{N}]^{N-x}
.
In this pattern the last opening bracket that is not closed is the last bracket of the
x
-th
w_{k}
after
u
.
Recall that configurations
d_{x,l}
were obtained by pumping inside the
x
-th
w_{k}
, so before this bracket;
for
l\to\infty
the number of stars inserted there is unbounded.
From the definition of the language
U
it follows that the sequence
\sharp(S_{1}),\sharp(S_{2}),\dots
has to be unbounded.
On the other hand, configurations
d_{y,l}
were obtained by pumping inside the
y
-th
w_{k}
, so
after the last opening bracket that was not closed (as
y>x
).
For each
l
the number of stars before this bracket is the same.
From the definition of the language
U
it follows that the sequence
\sharp(T_{1}),\sharp(T_{2}),\dots
has to be constant, hence bounded.
This contradicts the thesis of Theorem
12
, which says that these sequences are either both bounded or both unbounded.
∎
